The Physical Body

2 The Physical Body

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Physical AI stack focus for this chapter.

Why can a correctly chosen action still demand more than the machine’s body can deliver?

A mobile manipulator can recognize an obstacle correctly and still strike it if its body cannot stop within the remaining distance. The same machine can hold a payload without moving while its motor windings overheat, or lose its controller when a sudden demand for current pulls the supply voltage too low. These failures arise from limits that prediction accuracy alone cannot reveal. Physical AI engineering requires connecting a proposed action to the motion, heat, and electrical demand it creates, then establishing which limit the assembled machine reaches first. Measurements must also establish the conditions under which those limits hold, because a change in payload, floor surface, cooling, or battery state changes what the machine can deliver. The body’s limits therefore govern what the permission path may admit before a command crosses the causal boundary and produces irreversible physical work.

Learning Objectives
  • Calculate observation freshness limits from measurement age and the machine’s allowable motion
  • Calculate stopping distance and the admissible speed ceiling from pre-brake delay, credible deceleration, and clearance overheads
  • Evaluate actuator and transmission choices against response limits, reflected inertia, and mechanical impedance
  • Estimate sustainable motor duty cycles from heat generation, cooling, and operating conditions
  • Budget electrical supply margins for simultaneous actuation, computation, and regenerative braking
  • Specify the elements of a limit record that states a measured limit’s conditions, uncertainty, margin, runtime detector, and evidence category
  • Evaluate maneuver-specific binding budgets from qualified evidence and justify holding up the permission supply through covered faults
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

2.1 The Five Physical Budgets

Every action a learned model proposes ultimately manifests as electrical current flowing through a motor winding, and from that instant physics governs execution. Machine learning practice rarely contends with this transition. Policies trained in simulation typically interact with idealized plants where commanded torque appears instantaneously, heat never accumulates in windings, and direct current (DC) bus voltages never sag under load. A physical machine honors none of these simplifications. The physical body is the coupled assembly of actuators, mechanical transmissions, structural linkages, power electronics, and sensors that translates digital commands into mechanical motion. Its physical dynamics bound what even a mathematically optimal policy can achieve. An emergency brake command requires finite time to develop mechanical retarding force and clearance distance to dissipate kinetic energy. Similarly, commanding a manipulator to hold a heavy payload motionless draws continuous phase current, dissipating resistive heat into the stator even though the joint does zero mechanical work.

The proposal boundary in The Machine in Five Levels assigns the permission path responsibility for admitting action, and the body provides the concrete physical envelopes that make that gating decision meaningful. Before committing a command to hardware registers, the permission path must evaluate five physical budgets. It must confirm that the sensor observation remains fresh, that the moving machine can stop within available clearance, that the actuator can deliver the required torque trajectory, that the winding can tolerate the resulting thermal rise, and that the electrical bus can support the transient current demand. These mechanisms form a direct causal chain. Sensor and pipeline latencies consume allowable stopping distance. Arresting momentum demands delivered actuator torque. Generating that torque dissipates heat in motor coils and pulls current from a shared power rail. For any given maneuver, the governing systems question is which of these physical budgets binds first, under what operational stress, and on what empirical evidence.

  1. Measurement freshness (\(\ObservationAge\)): The observation must remain valid until the mechanical plant responds. Latency accumulated across sensor exposure, data transport, neural inference, and low-level drive dispatch consumes allowable tracking margins as the physical world continues to move.
  2. Kinetic momentum (\(\KineticEnergy, \LinearMomentum\)): A moving machine carries kinetic energy (\(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \BodySpeed^2\)) and linear or angular momentum (\(\LinearMomentum = \Mass \BodySpeed\)). Bringing the plant to rest demands applying retarding force over finite distance and time; safe operating velocity must therefore preserve a stopping envelope well within available physical clearance.
  3. Actuator bandwidth and reflected inertia (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\)): Winding inductance and supply voltage limit the rate at which torque can change. A gearhead with reduction ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\) multiplies delivered torque by \(\GearReductionRatio\), but it also scales the motor rotor’s inertia \(\RotorInertia\) by \(\GearReductionRatio^2\) as seen at the joint output (section 2.4.3), which constrains instantaneous acceleration.
  4. Thermal capacity (\(\EstimatedWindingTemperature\)): Resistive losses (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\), with \(\EffectiveLossResistance\) the winding’s effective loss resistance) deposit thermal energy directly into the stator windings and inverter switching stages. Heat dissipation limits sustained continuous torque, while the thermal mass of the coils constrains the magnitude, duration, and repetition rate of transient torque bursts.
  5. Electrical power integrity (\(\DcRailVoltage\)): Onboard compute clusters and motor inverters draw from a shared DC power rail. Peak motor accelerations must not drag the bus voltage below digital logic thresholds, while regenerative braking must provide an electrical sink so returned kinetic energy does not overvoltage the bus.

Under the simultaneous admission invariant, motion permission requires a non-negative margin in every budget before the permission path commits torque to hardware registers. Each margin must be checked in its own units; time, distance, torque, temperature, and voltage are separate constraints, not interchangeable quantities: \[\begin{aligned} \ObservationAge &\le \TelemetryFreshnessBound, \\ \StoppingDistance &\le \ClearDistance, \\ |\CommandedJointTorque| &\le \OutputTorqueCeiling, \\ \EstimatedWindingTemperature &\le \WindingTemperatureLimit, \\ \MinimumRailVoltage &\ge \ConverterLockoutVoltage. \end{aligned}\] Here \(\CommandedJointTorque\) is requested torque and \(\OutputTorqueCeiling\) is the maximum deliverable output torque under current electrical and thermal conditions. The estimated winding temperature \(\EstimatedWindingTemperature\) must remain below its limit, while the lowest rail voltage \(\MinimumRailVoltage\) must remain above the undervoltage-lockout threshold \(\ConverterLockoutVoltage\) at which the supply regulator shuts down. The fifth line checks only undervoltage. Permission must also require regenerative braking to stay below the qualified upper operating-voltage limit of the supplied components, with an available absorption or dissipation path. Two lines state their budgets in derived form. The kinetic budget appears as the stopping distance it demands, \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\). The actuator line checks only torque magnitude, because the slew-rate limit that winding inductance and reflected inertia impose enters the stopping line instead, through the brake-onset term of the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\). All five inequalities must hold simultaneously. A surplus in four budgets cannot compensate for a deficit in the fifth. A fresh observation cannot prevent a stop from overrunning if kinetic energy exceeds available clearance, and abundant battery energy cannot prevent an actuator from burning out if thermal dissipation limits are violated.

Systems Perspective 2.1: Why physical limits bound physical AI architecture
The permission path requires concrete bounds on physical behavior, not abstract software promises. Accepting a torque command does not guarantee that the inverter can deliver the requested current against the back electromotive force (back-EMF) the spinning motor induces in its own windings, nor does cutting motor power guarantee that friction will arrest the plant before it breaches a clearance zone. The five physical budgets bridge this gap, tying software proposals to verifiable, empirical responses under declared operating conditions.

The warehouse mobile manipulator that this book follows draws on all five. Loaded to at most 368 kg and moving at its 1.5 m/s drive limit (illustrative; see the Reader Guide), it carries 414 J of kinetic energy through aisles it shares with people. Each budget establishes an empirical operating boundary on the assembled machine under declared thermal and electrical stress. Each ends the chapter as a limit record, the six-element record that The Architecture of This Book sketched from the stopping notebook: a value, the conditions under which it holds, its uncertainty, the margin defended, a runtime detector, and the provenance of its evidence. The first budget is the age of the measurement on which every command rests, which runs from physical transduction, well before any software process receives the raw sensor data.

2.2 Measurement Freshness

Software benchmarks in machine learning frequently report latency as isolated accelerator execution time, for example 18.5 ms for the vision backbone of the arm’s wrist-camera grasp path on an edge neural processing unit (NPU), a different pipeline from the navigation backbone that the stopping budget of Delay consumes stopping clearance charges. This narrow focus fosters the illusion of an agile 54.1 Hz control loop. In physical AI systems, however, that compute kernel represents only one intermediate segment in an end-to-end silicon-to-plant dataflow pipeline. A sensor measurement begins decaying the moment physical transduction occurs; what governs physical performance is its total age when torque actually appears at the actuator joint. That physical age spans the entire interval from the moment photons strike a photodiode array, magnetic flux sweeps past a Hall sensor, or strain deflects a piezoresistor to the moment motor inverter switches build stator magnetic flux that alters link acceleration. If a perception policy evaluates an obstacle bounding box in 18.5 ms, while exposure integration, sensor readout, Peripheral Component Interconnect Express (PCIe) transport, kernel scheduling, fieldbus dispatch, and motor current rise consume an additional 16.5 ms at the median, the true physical age of the observation when the joint accelerates is 35 ms. The distribution tail extends farther still.

Throughout that capture-to-onset window, the physical environment continues to evolve unobserved. In distributed data systems, read-after-write consistency windows govern whether a client observes stale or linearized state. In physical AI, the physical plant acts as an autonomous, unpausable concurrent writer: spatial position \(\CartesianPosition(\TimeInstant)\) evolves along continuous trajectories at velocity \(\Velocity(\TimeInstant)\). An observation age \(\ObservationAge\) converts directly into unobserved physical displacement \(\FiniteChange \CartesianPosition \approx \int_0^{\ObservationAge} \Velocity(\TimeInstant)\,d\TimeInstant\). High-speed machines inevitably act on historical state.

The rate of world-state change dictates how long a measurement remains valid before acting on it introduces intolerable error. Let \(\PositionErrorCeiling\) denote the maximum acceptable error in a tracked state, such as a joint angle or the position of a part on a conveyor, and let \(\SpeedBound\) denote the fastest credible velocity at which that state can evolve. The freshness deadline \(\TelemetryFreshnessBound\) is the upper bound on measurement age before a control action based on that measurement becomes invalid, defined by the inequality \[\TelemetryFreshnessBound \le \frac{\PositionErrorCeiling}{\SpeedBound} \tag{1}\]

Definition 2.1: Freshness deadline

Freshness deadline (\(\TelemetryFreshnessBound\)) is the largest age, counted from the capture of a measurement to the onset of the actuator’s response, that the measurement of a moving target may reach before the target’s motion alone spends the allowable position error \(\PositionErrorCeiling\) (equation 1).

  1. Significance: It converts a spatial tolerance into a time budget that every stage from exposure to torque onset must share. A 12 mm tolerance on a target moving at 0.20 m/s leaves 60 ms for sensing, inference, transport, and current rise together.
  2. Distinction: Unlike an inference-latency budget, which times one stage, the deadline bounds the age at onset; unlike a control-loop period, it is set by the target’s speed and the tolerance rather than by the loop rate. When the machine moves toward a stationary obstacle instead, the same age becomes the first term of the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) and is paid as blind travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay\) in the stopping distance.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the deadline as a property of the sensor or the model. It changes whenever the target moves faster or the tolerance shrinks, so a pipeline qualified on a slow conveyor can miss on a faster one without any change in its own timing.

↪ The same deadline applied to a stored belief, which lapses once its region falls out of view, is developed in Forward State Propagation and Occlusion Decay.

The running machine meets this bound at its pick station, where the arm grasps a mug from a moving conveyor. The belt carries the mug at speeds up to \(\ConveyorSpeed =\) 0.20 m/s, and the gripper mechanism tolerates \(\PositionalTaskTolerance =\) 15 mm of position error. Initial handle-pose estimation already consumes \(\InitialTrackingError =\) 3 mm of this margin. Consequently, the mug’s physical motion may spend at most \(\PositionErrorCeiling = \PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError =\) 12 mm, with \(\SpeedBound = \ConveyorSpeed\). Notebook 2.1 evaluates this resulting deadline against the ages an integrated camera-to-joint pipeline delivers in practice. The deadline governs any target that moves while its observation ages. For moving workpieces, it limits motion-induced tracking drift under bounded relative velocity and valid initial observations. Sensing and state-estimation noise consume separate slices of the tolerance budget, just as \(\InitialTrackingError\) does here. Conversely, when the robot approaches a stationary obstacle, such as a person standing in a warehouse aisle, the target does not move. Instead, the mobile base drives toward the person during the latency interval. Observation age then converts directly into unguided vehicular travel before braking begins, which section 2.3 charges against the kinetic stopping envelope.

2.2.1 Transduction physics, quantization noise, and Nyquist limits

If a 12-bit magnetic encoder jumps by a single count between two 1 ms servo ticks, backward differencing reports a sudden 88°/s velocity spike. If an unshielded cooling fan vibrates an inertial unit above half its sampling rate, the vibration folds into the low-frequency band and looks like chassis acceleration. Every sensor trades error tolerance against time: it must resolve the physical state of interest, and the analog filtering, sampling, or serial scanning needed to extract a clean signal must fit inside the freshness deadline. Four physical mechanisms set this trade:

  1. Optical Encoders and Velocity Quantization Noise: An encoder measures joint position in discrete angular increments. An \(\EncoderBitCount\)-bit encoder discretizes the continuous \(2\pi\text{ rad}\) circle into an angular resolution of \(\QuantizationStep \EncoderAngle = \frac{2\pi}{2^\EncoderBitCount}\). For an 18-bit absolute optical encoder, \(\QuantizationStep \EncoderAngle \approx 2.4 \times 10^{-5}\text{ rad}\) (\(0.0014^\circ\)); for a 12-bit magnetic encoder, \(\QuantizationStep \EncoderAngle \approx 1.53 \times 10^{-3}\text{ rad}\) (\(0.088^\circ\)). Feedback controllers, which compute each command from the measured difference between the desired and the actual state, estimate joint velocity from successive position samples across a discrete control cycle \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\): \[{\EncoderVelocityEstimate{}}_{k} \approx \frac{{\EncoderAngle}_{k} - {\EncoderAngle}_{k-1}}{\FiniteChange \TimeInstant}\] Backward differencing acts as an effective high-pass filter, scaling high-frequency position fluctuations by \(1/\FiniteChange \TimeInstant\). Under an idealized model where successive quantization errors are independent and uniformly distributed across one encoder count, each sample has variance \((\QuantizationStep\EncoderAngle)^2/12\). Differencing two independent samples sums their variances to \((\QuantizationStep\EncoderAngle)^2/6\), yielding a velocity-error standard deviation of \(\EncoderVelocityNoise = \frac{\QuantizationStep\EncoderAngle}{\sqrt{6}\,\FiniteChange \TimeInstant}\). At a \(1\text{ kHz}\) servo update rate (\(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant = 1\text{ ms}\)), a single-count jump on a 12-bit encoder (\(1.53\text{ mrad}\)) registers as an abrupt velocity spike of \(1.53\text{ rad/s}\) (\(88^\circ/\text{s}\)). If routed directly into velocity feedback loops or neural policy state inputs, these discrete impulses cause severe torque chatter and excite mechanical resonances. Downstream low-pass filters or state observers (models that infer a quantity such as velocity from a stream of measurements) smooth this quantization noise, but filter group delay introduces phase lag, consuming milliseconds of the freshness budget.

  2. Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) and Nyquist Aliasing: An inertial measurement unit measures linear acceleration and angular velocity. Sampling must isolate macroscopic body dynamics from high-frequency structural vibration. Under the Nyquist-Shannon sampling theorem (Nyquist 1928; Shannon 1949), continuous signals require a sampling frequency strictly greater than twice their highest spectral component (\(\SamplingRate > 2 \HighestSignalFrequency\)). Mechanical sources, including gear mesh harmonics, cooling fans, and motor inverter switching, generate vibrations reaching kilohertz frequencies. Without adequate analog anti-aliasing filters before the analog-to-digital converter (ADC), structural vibrations above the Nyquist frequency fold back into baseband frequencies (\(1\text{--}20\text{ Hz}\)). The state estimator, which combines sensor readings into an estimate of the machine’s motion, cannot distinguish these aliased artifacts from genuine chassis acceleration, introducing cumulative drift into dead-reckoning integrations. Raising downstream software loop rates cannot reconstruct information already corrupted at the sensor hardware interface.

  3. Tactile Sensor Arrays and Scanning Bandwidth: Tactile arrays measure contact pressure across a robot gripper using discrete sensing elements called taxels. To minimize cabling across robotic wrists, hardware architectures multiplex taxels across shared row and column lines. Scanning these elements sequentially forces an entire frame to span the multiplexing period. This serial scan time adds directly to the measurement age and limits the bandwidth for detecting rapid contact events, such as the onset of localized slip. Increasing the taxel density without scaling the underlying ADC readout rate produces older tactile observations.

  4. Cameras and Rolling-Shutter Readout: A rolling-shutter CMOS camera exposes and transfers pixel rows sequentially, so a single frame spans its entire readout duration. Image timestamps must therefore anchor to a defined reference, such as the exposure midpoint of a designated row, rather than the frame-arrival interrupt.

Across all four mechanisms, a measurement begins aging at physical transduction, milliseconds before any software driver receives a buffer. Bounding how much of the freshness deadline remains at actuation therefore requires tracing the signal across every stage between transduction and motor current.

↪ Rolling-shutter distortion on the navigation camera, and its compensation before images enter the state estimator, is developed in Capture Physics and Calibration.

2.2.2 The end-to-end sense-to-actuation latency pipeline

A measurement’s physical age begins at capture, the moment of physical transduction, rather than when an operating system process first receives the data buffer. Three subsequent boundary events demarcate the pipeline from transduction to mechanical work. Dispatch is when perception publishes dated perceptual evidence to the action proposer. Admission is when the real-time permission path validates and accepts a proposal built using that evidence. Onset is when electrical current in the motor stator windings begins driving physical acceleration at the joint. The four events recur throughout the book, and The capture-to-brake-onset timing budget of the running machine later budgets the intervals between them for the running machine. For the freshness deadline, capture and onset form the bounding endpoints, and the deadline limits the cumulative delay between them.

An illustrative camera-to-joint pipeline reveals how latency compounds across physical and digital boundaries (table 1). Between capture and dispatch, the CMOS sensor integrates exposure and reads out raw scanlines over MIPI CSI-2.1 Direct memory access (DMA) transfers the frame into host memory, and the vision backbone executes. In this representative architecture, the policy network executes in the same inference pass as the backbone, spanning the dispatch boundary. Between dispatch and admission, inter-process communication (IPC) transmits the proposed trajectory to the safety microcontroller, a dedicated real-time processor that executes the deterministic permission path. Between admission and onset, an EtherCAT fieldbus2 delivers target setpoints to individual motor drives, where power-inverter gate drivers switch to ramp up stator current. Neural inference occupies one of the eight stages. Under memory bus contention and scheduling delays, the peripheral system stages expand the latency tail substantially more than the neural network execution itself.

1 MIPI CSI-2 (Camera Serial Interface 2): The Mobile Industry Processor Interface camera link carries raw frames from the image sensor to the system-on-chip and marks the start and end of each frame with short packets. Latching a hardware timestamp on the frame-start packet ties the frame to a fixed offset from its exposure rather than to the moment software first sees it, which is what lets transduction time, not arrival time, anchor the age budget.

2 EtherCAT: An industrial Ethernet fieldbus in which one frame passes through every drive on the segment, each node reading and writing its own slice of the frame as it passes. Frame length and node count, not contention for the wire, fix the cycle time, which is why the fieldbus row of table 1 has equal \(\text{p50}\) and \(\text{p99}\). Comparing bus occupancy on CAN FD and EtherCAT sizes that cycle for the running machine.

3 Quantile Convolution: In general, the quantile of a sum of delays does not equal the sum of their individual quantiles (\(\LatencyQuantileFunction(\UpstreamStageLatency + \DownstreamStageLatency) \ne \LatencyQuantileFunction(\UpstreamStageLatency) + \LatencyQuantileFunction(\DownstreamStageLatency)\)). Even when stages are independent, adding their \(\text{p99}\) values is not a worst-case bound, and shared buses and queues correlate the stages so that the end-to-end tail must be measured across the whole pipeline.

Systems engineers sometimes estimate worst-case pipeline latency by summing the 99th-percentile (\(\text{p99}\)) latencies of individual stages. However, the percentile of a sum does not equal the sum of the percentiles.3 In embedded system-on-chip (SoC) architectures, shared hardware resources couple stage delays. For example, PCIe DMA bursts during camera readout contend for memory-controller bandwidth with neural accelerators, while CPU cache evictions during kernel message passing induce thread scheduling jitter. The end-to-end distribution of sense-to-torque latency must therefore be profiled empirically on the integrated hardware platform under concurrent production workloads. Furthermore, an empirical percentile remains a statistical measurement; it does not replace the formal worst-case bounds required for hard real-time safety guarantees. Under continuous inference load, each stage in table 1 carries its own tail, and the two bottom rows separate the arithmetic column sums from the end-to-end age the system actually delivers.

The 50 ms sum of individual stage percentiles overstates the system-level \(\text{p99}\) latency of 46.2 ms because pipeline stages rarely experience worst-case contention simultaneously. For the same reason, the sum of the stage maxima enters the end-to-end row only as an upper bound within the stated illustrative profile and conditions. A measured maximum needs a separately justified allowance or bound before it can support a hard real-time deadline. Coupling pushes the other way. Stages that slow down together, because they contend for the same memory controller or scheduler, fatten the end-to-end tail toward the stage sum, while stages that vary independently usually leave that sum pessimistic, as here. The end-to-end median in the table is set equal to the stage-median sum, a simplification of the illustrative profile that a measured one need not share. Notebook 2.1 evaluates how this illustrative profile behaves against the conveyor’s physical deadline.

Napkin Math 2.1: The freshness deadline
Problem: The arm grasps a mug riding the pick-station conveyor, acting on camera observations of the handle. Suppose the grasp path had the illustrative age profile of table 1. Does it deliver those observations within the freshness deadline, and how far does the mug move at the median and at the tail of the observation age?

Variables:

  • Grasp tolerance \(\PositionalTaskTolerance =\) 15 mm, of which the initial estimate spends \(\InitialTrackingError =\) 3 mm (illustrative)
  • Conveyor speed \(\SpeedBound = \ConveyorSpeed =\) 0.20 m/s (illustrative)
  • Median backbone time 18.5 ms, median end-to-end age 35 ms, and \(\text{p99.9}\) age 68.4 ms (illustrative profile of table 1)

Math:

  1. Deadline: \(\PositionErrorCeiling = \PositionalTaskTolerance - \InitialTrackingError =\) 12 mm, so \(\TelemetryFreshnessBound = \PositionErrorCeiling/\SpeedBound =\) 12 mm \(/\) 0.20 m/s \(=\) 60 ms (equation 1).
  2. Backbone alone: 18.5 ms is less than 60 ms, so the one stage a benchmark times fits with room to spare.
  3. Median chain: 0.20 m/s \(\times\) 35 ms \(=\) 7 mm of mug travel, 5 mm inside the tolerance.
  4. Tail: 0.20 m/s \(\times\) 68.4 ms \(=\) 13.7 mm, 1.7 mm past the tolerance.

Result: Inference and the median chain both meet the deadline, but the stated \(\text{p99.9}\) age exceeds it. The profile therefore does not establish that 99.9 percent of grasps meet the freshness deadline.

Systems insight: The whole path from exposure to torque onset spends the freshness deadline, not the model alone, and its tail, not its median, decides whether the grasp holds. When the machine instead drives toward a stationary obstacle, the same age enters the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\) as its first term and is paid as blind travel \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay\), the first term of the stopping distance that section 2.3 sets against the clear distance.

Excessive feedback delay can also degrade closed-loop tracking or destabilize motor control loops,4 but the freshness calculation requires no frequency-domain model: at the latency tail, the mug has already moved beyond mechanical capture tolerance. System architectures must therefore preserve the observation’s true physical capture timestamp. Hardware timers synchronized through IEEE 802.1AS, the generalized Precision Time Protocol (gPTP),5 or hardware capture strobes associated with the MIPI CSI-2 receiver provide a common physical timebase. The sensor’s exposure and readout characteristics determine how that hardware timestamp maps to the measurement midpoint. Once latched, this capture timestamp accompanies the observation through every subsequent compute kernel.

4 Transport Delay and Feedback Stability: Transport delay makes a controller act on historical state. Whether delayed corrections destabilize the loop depends on the plant, controller, and total response. Discrete-time sampling and zero-order hold phase lag develops the phase-margin calculation; delay alone does not establish a particular oscillation or damage outcome.

5 IEEE 802.1AS (gPTP): The generalized Precision Time Protocol of IEEE 802.1AS is a profile of the IEEE 1588 Precision Time Protocol (IEEE 2020a) for time-sensitive Ethernet networks (IEEE 2020b). It synchronizes clocks by exchanging packets timestamped in hardware at the network interface, which keeps the operating-system scheduler’s jitter out of the measurement. The age \(\ObservationAge = \CurrentWallClockTime - \TransductionInstant\) means something only when the camera, the host, and the drives read one timebase; an offset between their clocks enters the freshness budget as an error that no timestamp reveals.

IEEE. 2020a. IEEE Std 1588-2019: IEEE Standard for a Precision Clock Synchronization Protocol for Networked Measurement and Control Systems. Institute of Electrical; Electronics Engineers. https://doi.org/10.1109/IEEESTD.2020.9120376.
IEEE. 2020b. IEEE Std 802.1AS-2020: IEEE Standard for Local and Metropolitan Area Networks, Timing and Synchronization for Time-Sensitive Applications. Institute of Electrical; Electronics Engineers.
Brooks, Rodney. 1986. “A Robust Layered Control System for a Mobile Robot.” IEEE Journal on Robotics and Automation 2 (1): 14–23.

At the admission boundary, the real-time permission path computes the observation’s total elapsed age, \(\ObservationAge = \CurrentWallClockTime - \TransductionInstant\), and adds the bounded remaining admission-to-onset interval for fieldbus dispatch and motor current rise. Capture and clock-synchronization uncertainty must also fit the qualified age budget. The permission path rejects the proposed trajectory update if its projected onset age exceeds the freshness deadline. Rejecting a stale trajectory replacement does not require cutting motor torque immediately; an executing command may continue while its qualified physical margin remains valid. If that margin expires before fresh, valid observations arrive, the permission path triggers a qualified fallback maneuver within the remaining stopping distance (Brooks 1986).

Systems Perspective 2.2: The publish timestamp anti-pattern
A widespread architectural bug in robotics systems is timestamping sensor observations inside user-space publishers, such as a Robot Operating System 2 (ROS 2) node transmitting over Data Distribution Service (DDS) middleware. Latching timestamps in application software discards the duration of sensor exposure, rolling-shutter integration, sensor readout, DMA transfers, and kernel queueing. The latency budget therefore loses stages 1 through 4 of table 1 (exposure, readout, transport, and DMA), along with any kernel queueing before the publisher runs. Hardware strobes must latch timestamps directly at the physical silicon interface (such as MIPI CSI-2 Start-of-Frame interrupts or Ethernet physical-layer transceiver timestamps synchronized via IEEE 802.1AS) before data enters operating-system queues.

Bounding measurement age ensures that control policies act on valid observations rather than historical artifacts, but freshness alone cannot guarantee collision avoidance. Throughout that capture-to-onset interval, the body continues moving under unguided momentum, consuming physical clearance before braking can begin. Halting that physical mass requires dissipating its stored kinetic energy across real distance, an irreversible mechanical process governed by momentum rather than processor clock rates.

2.3 Kinetic Momentum

When the loaded mobile manipulator drives down a warehouse aisle at its 1.5 m/s drive limit and a pedestrian steps out from behind a rack 1.10 m ahead, the capture-to-onset age from section 2.2 becomes unguided travel toward the pedestrian. Even after the brake command arrives, halting the base is not instantaneous: the moving chassis carries linear momentum (\(\LinearMomentum = \Mass \SpeedAtDetection\)) and stores kinetic energy (\(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2}\Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2\)) that must be dissipated across real space through mechanical work (\(\MechanicalWork = \int \AppliedForce \, d\TravelPosition\)). Stopping distance grows linearly with pre-brake delay but quadratically with velocity, so above the crossover speed of the elementary stopping budget, the braking term rather than the delay dominates the stop.

2.3.1 Kinematic stopping distance and pre-brake delay

As in the elementary stopping budget, a stop divides into two phases, pre-brake delay and active braking. During the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\), the vehicle travels forward without any retarding force. This latency accounts for the age of the sensory observation when perception hands it downstream, followed by the communication transit, safety validation, and actuator-onset intervals required before mechanical braking begins. It excludes the dispatch-to-admission interval, in which the proposer and the permission path turn that observation into an admitted command, because Sizing the composite stopping budget charges that interval separately. An independent protective response need not wait for a motion planning proposal; safety enforcement can trigger immediately along a fast-path monitor. The safety budget must trace whichever concrete execution path initiates braking. Furthermore, if an obstacle can enter the path between sensing cycles, the budget must include an allowance for camera exposure and sampling intervals. The budget conservatively credits zero retarding force during this total pre-brake delay and assumes approximately constant forward speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\), which gives the blind travel distance \[\BlindTravelDistance = \SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay\]

↩︎ The two-phase stopping budget is established in Delay consumes stopping clearance.

Once the selected braking mechanism engages, the system enters the active braking phase. Mechanical work done by the retarding braking force \(\BrakingForce\) over the physical braking distance \(\BrakingDistance\) must dissipate the machine’s stored kinetic energy, \(\MechanicalWork = \FiniteChange \KineticEnergy\). How that retarding force arises depends on the physical hardware. A powered motor stop requires functioning drive inverters, sufficient bus-voltage headroom, and a circuit capable of absorbing or dissipating returned electrical power. A mechanical brake, by contrast, operates independently of electrical power but carries distinct engagement latencies and thermal dissipation limits. In any safety certification, the credible deceleration must reflect the specific mechanism available during the fault scenario under analysis.

In an embodied machine, stored kinetic energy includes rotating powertrain components as well as the translating chassis. A component spinning at angular velocity \(\RotatingBodyAngularSpeed\) stores rotational energy \(\frac{1}{2} \RotatingBodyInertia \RotatingBodyAngularSpeed^2\), where its moment of inertia \(\RotatingBodyInertia\) (in \(\text{kg}\cdot\text{m}^2\)) measures resistance to rotational acceleration. It plays the same role that mass plays in translational energy \(\frac{1}{2} \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2\). A transmission with gear ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\) turns the motor shaft \(\GearReductionRatio\) times for every revolution of the wheel, multiplying motor torque by \(\GearReductionRatio\) while dividing speed by \(\GearReductionRatio\). The machine’s total kinetic energy sums the translational energy of the chassis and payload (\(\TranslationalKineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2\)) and the rotational energy of its wheels, gearboxes, and motor rotors (\(\RotationalKineticEnergy = \sum_i \frac{1}{2} {\RotorInertia}_i {\RotorAngularSpeed}_i^2\)). Because a drive with gear ratio \({\GearReductionRatio}_i\) spins its rotor at angular speed \({\RotorAngularSpeed}_i = {\GearReductionRatio}_i \frac{\SpeedAtDetection}{\WheelRadius}\), the rotor’s rotational kinetic energy reflects into the translational frame scaled by the square of the gear ratio: \[\TotalKineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2 + \sum_i \frac{1}{2} {\RotorInertia}_i \left({\GearReductionRatio}_i \frac{\SpeedAtDetection}{\WheelRadius}\right)^2 = \frac{1}{2} \left(\Mass + \sum_i \frac{{\GearReductionRatio}_i^2 {\RotorInertia}_i}{\WheelRadius^2}\right) \SpeedAtDetection^2\] This formulation reveals that the effective translational inertia of the machine is \(\EffectiveImpactMass = \Mass + \sum_i \frac{{\GearReductionRatio}_i^2 {\RotorInertia}_i}{\WheelRadius^2}\). For a platform decelerating to rest under sustained retarding force \(\BrakingForce = \EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration\), the work-energy theorem balances mechanical work against total kinetic energy: \[\EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \BrakingDistance = \frac{1}{2} \EffectiveImpactMass \SpeedAtDetection^2\] Dividing both sides by effective mass yields the kinematic braking travel: \[\BrakingDistance = \frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration}\] If an engineer evaluates braking capacity using bare chassis mass alone (\(\NominalBrakingDeceleration = \BrakingForce / \Mass\)), they omit the kinetic energy stored in the spinning motor rotors. The result overestimates deceleration by the factor \(\EffectiveImpactMass / \Mass\) and underestimates stopping travel by \(\frac{\EffectiveImpactMass - \Mass}{\Mass} = \sum_i \frac{{\GearReductionRatio}_i^2 {\RotorInertia}_i}{\Mass \WheelRadius^2}\). On the running machine, this correction remains modest. Its two driven wheels use direct-drive hub motors (\({\GearReductionRatio}_i = 1\)), and because no portion of a hub motor lies outside its tire circumference, each rotor term \({\RotorInertia}_i/\WheelRadius^2\) cannot exceed the motor’s 9.5 kg total mass. The rotating parts add at most 5.2 percent to the 368 kg loaded mass. However, because the inertia reflection scales with \({\GearReductionRatio}_i^2\), this rotational overhead becomes substantial on lightweight robot platforms driven through high-ratio planetary or harmonic gearboxes.

The parameter \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) is the credible sustained deceleration the physical plant can reliably exert against the environment, not an idealized rating copied from an actuator datasheet. The retarding force \(\BrakingForce = \EffectiveImpactMass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) must fit beneath three independent physical ceilings. First, tire-floor traction imposes a strict friction ceiling. Only the braked wheels transmit retarding shear to the ground, and friction there can reach at most \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) times the normal force they support, \(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction \Mass \Gravity\). This caps deceleration at \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration \le \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity \DrivenWheelLoadFraction\), where \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\) is the tire-floor friction coefficient and \(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction\) is the fraction of total vehicle weight carried by the driven wheels. Second, vehicle pitch stability limits deceleration through dynamic load transfer. While braking force acts at the floor contact patch, inertial force acts forward through the center of gravity at height \(\GravityCenterHeight\) above it. Taking moments about the leading ground support, a horizontal distance \(\LeadingSupportDistance\) ahead of the center of gravity, the overturning inertial moment \(\Mass \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \GravityCenterHeight\) opposes the restoring gravitational moment \(\Mass \Gravity \LeadingSupportDistance\). The platform stays on all wheels only so long as \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration \le \Gravity\,\LeadingSupportDistance/\GravityCenterHeight\). Raising or extending a robotic arm alters both \(\GravityCenterHeight\) and \(\LeadingSupportDistance\) in real time, making this pitch-stability boundary state-dependent. Third, the electromechanical actuators limit retarding force through the maximum torque their inverter current limit allows, a ceiling that section 2.4.2 derives from the motor torque constant. If an inverter restricts phase current, or if the back-EMF of the spinning rotor leaves the inverter too little headroom to control current, the drive cannot deliver the required braking torque. Deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) degrades silently, expanding braking travel without software notification. The rotor’s rotational inertia is already counted in the effective mass \(\EffectiveImpactMass\) and must not be charged a second time. These three ceilings act simultaneously on the loaded chassis (figure 1), and notebook 2.3 checks them against the running machine’s numbers once the next section derives the drive’s torque ceiling.

The constant-deceleration model requires a level surface and a qualified braking response. A conservative implementation charges the transient interval required to develop full retarding force into \(\PrebrakeDelay\), then applies a certified lower bound \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\). The actuator brake-onset allowance (\(\ActuatorResponseTime\)) enters that delay once. Any subsequent calculation that models a finite torque ramp must reconcile its ramp travel with this allowance rather than add the same interval twice. Furthermore, empirical stopping trials across declared operating conditions establish the credible deceleration, its distribution, and its safety margin. A sample percentile alone does not constitute a certified lower bound.

In unstructured environments, an autonomous machine cannot allocate its entire clearance to nominal stopping dynamics. Obstacle tracking carries state estimation uncertainty, sensor calibration drifts over time, and localization algorithms introduce position error. The stopping distance \(\StoppingDistance\) that the system defends must therefore include a spatial localization uncertainty bound \(\LocalizationBound\) and a minimum physical clearance margin \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\):6 \[\StoppingDistance = \SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay + \frac{\SpeedAtDetection^2}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration} + \LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin \tag{2}\] The sum \(\LocalizationBound + \ProtectiveClearanceMargin\) is fixed spatial overhead that must remain unoccupied when the body comes to a complete rest.

6 Protective Separation Distance: For speed and separation monitoring of robot arms, the collaborative-application requirements that ISO 10218-2:2025 incorporates from ISO/TS 15066 (which itself remains published and under revision) write the protective separation distance as \(\ProtectiveSeparationDistance = \PersonApproachTravel + \RobotReactionTravel + \RobotStoppingTravel + \IntrusionDistance + \PersonPositionUncertainty + \RobotPositionUncertainty\): the person’s travel, the robot’s travel during its reaction time and while stopping, an intrusion distance set by the sensing, and the position uncertainties of person and robot (International Organization for Standardization 2016, 2025; Marvel and Norcross 2017). The robot’s terms map onto equation 2, with \(\SpeedAtDetection\,\PrebrakeDelay\) as \(\RobotReactionTravel\), \(\SpeedAtDetection^2/2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) as \(\RobotStoppingTravel\), and \(\LocalizationBound\) as \(\RobotPositionUncertainty\), although ISO 10218-2:2025 excludes the hazards specific to a manipulator riding a mobile platform, the running machine’s case. For a learned policy, an unbounded compute tail lengthens the reaction time, which enlarges both \(\PersonApproachTravel\) and \(\RobotReactionTravel\) and forces the protective field outward.

International Organization for Standardization. 2016. ISO/TS 15066:2016: Robots and Robotic Devices, Collaborative Robots. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2025. ISO 10218-2:2025: Robotics, Safety Requirements, Part 2: Industrial Robot Applications and Robot Cells. International Organization for Standardization.
Marvel, Jeremy A., and Rick Norcross. 2017. “Implementing Speed and Separation Monitoring in Collaborative Robot Workcells.” Robotics and Computer-Integrated Manufacturing 44: 144–55. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.rcim.2016.08.001.

↪ The localization bound \(\LocalizationBound\) and its dependence on map corrections are developed in Localization drift and map frames that jump.

Definition 2.2: Dynamic stopping distance

Dynamic stopping distance (\(\StoppingDistance\)) is the minimum physical clearance required to bring a moving mass to a complete halt from its speed \(\SpeedAtDetection\) when the hazard is first imaged. It sums three parts. The first is the travel during the pre-brake delay \(\PrebrakeDelay\), which spans the observation age of the frame that shows the hazard and the lease, tick, bus, and brake-onset intervals that follow until retarding force begins (the decomposition of Delay consumes stopping clearance), and which excludes the separately charged dispatch-to-admission interval. The second is the braking travel under the credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\). The last is the localization bound \(\LocalizationBound\) and protective clearance \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\), as equation 2 states.

  1. Significance: Quantifies the non-negotiable physical spatial margin that software proposals must clear before motion permission can be granted.
  2. Distinction: Unlike static geometric obstacle clearance in classical kinematics, dynamic stopping distance depends on the machine’s own speed and on every delay before braking, so each velocity setpoint and each delay term changes it.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming an emergency stop command halts the machine instantaneously, ignoring that moving mass carries kinetic energy (\(\KineticEnergy = \frac{1}{2}\Mass \SpeedAtDetection^2\)) that requires finite mechanical work and braking distance to dissipate.

Three cases on the running machine show how the terms trade against one another (figure 2). With only the terms the body owns, the stop needs 742.5 mm. Charging the full 133.6 ms pre-brake delay of the elementary stopping budget (Delay consumes stopping clearance), whose terms later chapters measure one by one, gives that elementary stop plus the 150 mm fixed overhead, or 912.9 mm. That stop still fits the 1.10 m clear distance. Raising speed above the drive limit with that same delay pushes the stop to 1,417.2 mm, past the rack end, because speed enters the blind travel linearly and the braking travel quadratically. Notebook 2.2 works the first case term by term.

2.3.2 Dynamic velocity ceilings and clearance budgeting

Because braking travel grows quadratically with speed while blind travel scales linearly with delay (figure 2), autonomous systems cannot rely on static, scene-independent speed limits. They require a dynamic velocity ceiling parameterized by instantaneous obstacle clearance. For a stationary obstacle detected at distance \(\ClearDistance\), such as a person standing at an aisle intersection or a dropped storage tote, safe operation demands that defended stopping distance remain strictly within the available clear path: \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\). This constraint is how the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), binds every motion cycle, with each parameter in \(\StoppingDistance\) grounded in the machine’s verified physical limits. Using conservative bounds for actuation delay and retarding acceleration, the permission path solves this inequality to determine the maximum permissible command velocity \(\SpeedBound\). The symbol is the one that equation 1 used for the speed of a tracked target, but here it bounds the machine’s own speed. Taking the positive physical root of the resulting quadratic relation7 yields: \[\SpeedBound = -\CredibleBrakingDeceleration \PrebrakeDelay + \sqrt{(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration \PrebrakeDelay)^2 + 2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration (\ClearDistance - \LocalizationBound - \ProtectiveClearanceMargin)} \tag{3}\] Whenever perceptual range degrades or an obstacle obstructs the trajectory, \(\ClearDistance\) contracts, continuously lowering the velocity ceiling. When available distance collapses to zero, the allowable forward velocity becomes zero. If the machine is already in motion, however, issuing a zero-velocity setpoint cannot instantaneously eliminate its accumulated kinetic energy. A negative available distance signals that the platform has already breached safe stopping margins, demanding immediate, full-authority emergency braking.

7 Kinematic Velocity Root: Grouping the fixed distance terms into the available braking distance \(\AvailableBrakingDistance = \ClearDistance - \LocalizationBound - \ProtectiveClearanceMargin\) gives the quadratic inequality \(\frac{1}{2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration} \SpeedAtDetection^2 + \PrebrakeDelay \SpeedAtDetection - \AvailableBrakingDistance \le 0\). Multiplying by \(2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) gives \(\SpeedAtDetection^2 + 2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \PrebrakeDelay \SpeedAtDetection - 2 \CredibleBrakingDeceleration \AvailableBrakingDistance \le 0\). Applying the quadratic formula and taking the positive root sets the speed ceiling.

Napkin Math 2.2: The body's stopping distance
Problem: The loaded warehouse mobile manipulator drives toward a rack end at its drive limit, and a person stands in the aisle at the clear distance. The body owns three terms of equation 2: braking travel, the fixed overhead, and the brake onset that enters \(\PrebrakeDelay\). How much of the clear distance do these terms use, what speed would they alone permit, and how do speed and added delay change the answer?

Variables:

  • Loaded mass at most 368 kg (derived upper bound, full tote rack) and drive limit \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.5 m/s (illustrative)
  • Clear distance \(\ClearDistance =\) 1.10 m (illustrative)
  • Credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² (illustrative; loaded, dry floor)
  • Localization bound \(\LocalizationBound =\) 50 mm (illustrative) and protective clearance \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin =\) 100 mm (chosen)
  • Brake onset \(\ActuatorResponseTime =\) 20 ms (illustrative)

Math:

1. Braking travel: For the same credible deceleration, mass cancels from the work-energy balance. The loaded machine’s 414 J sets the energy the stopping system must absorb or dissipate, while the specified deceleration sets the braking distance. A different payload can change that deceleration and requires a new bound: \[d_{\text{brake}} = \frac{v^2}{2 a_{\text{brake}}} = \frac{(1.5 m/s)^2}{2 \times 2 m/s²} = 562.5 mm\] The stopping distance so far is 562.5 mm.

2. Fixed overhead: \[\delta_{\text{loc}} + \delta_{\text{margin}} = 50 mm + 100 mm = 150 mm\] The running total rises to 712.5 mm.

3. Brake onset: Between the brake command and the first retarding force, the base still moves at full speed: \[v\,T_{\text{act}} = (1.5 m/s) \times (20 ms) = 30 mm\] \[d_{\text{stop}}\big|_{\text{body}} = 562.5 mm + 150 mm + 30 mm = 742.5 mm\] The body’s own terms leave 357.5 mm of the 1.10 m clear distance for every delay the body does not own.

4. The body-only ceiling: Solving equation 2 for speed with \(\PrebrakeDelay = \ActuatorResponseTime\): \[v_{\max} = -a_{\text{brake}} T_{\text{act}} + \sqrt{(a_{\text{brake}} T_{\text{act}})^2 + 2 a_{\text{brake}} (D_{\text{clear}} - \delta_{\text{loc}} - \delta_{\text{margin}})} \approx 1.91 m/s\] The body alone would permit 1.91 m/s, above the drive limit. The remaining delay terms, added in later chapters, spend part of this headroom.

5. Speed vs. delay: At a hypothetical 2 m/s, above the drive limit, the braking term alone grows to 1,000 mm, 77.8 percent more than at the drive limit. At the drive limit, each additional 100 ms of pre-brake delay adds 150 mm.

Systems insight: Platform velocity is the most sensitive variable in the stopping budget. Accelerating from 1.5 m/s to 2 m/s adds 437.5 mm in braking travel, as much distance as 291.7 ms of additional pre-brake delay at the drive limit. A velocity setpoint may therefore rise only after re-evaluating the clearance quadratic. Because physical braking distance scales with \(\SpeedAtDetection^2\), granting an aggressive velocity setpoint expands the defended stopping envelope and rapidly exhausts the clear distance margin defended by \(\StoppingDistance \le \ClearDistance\).

↪ The chunk lease, the time an admitted trajectory segment may keep executing without a replacement, is one of the delay terms that spend this headroom. Its sizing from this stopping distance is developed in Chunk Lease Sizing from the Stopping Budget.

2.3.3 Applying the velocity ceiling

A dynamic speed ceiling protects the machine only when an independent check on the permission path tests each new velocity request against it before execution. The learned policy proposes an action. The safety check evaluates clearance, delay, and available deceleration to determine whether that request fits the stopping budget. Requesting a lower speed alters future trajectory points but cannot erase the distance required to stop from the current speed. Consequently, the check must evaluate the platform’s instantaneous state alongside candidate commands, keeping the machine inside the region from which it can still stop between control ticks under strict bounds on timing jitter, obstacle motion, and actuator response. That per-tick validation belongs to the permission path; Phase portraits and speed ceilings formalizes it around the jerk-limited deceleration profiles executed on the physical platform. What the physical body contributes is the ceiling itself: a clearance-dependent boundary grounded in physical constraints that drops whenever traction degrades, actuator response slows, or localization uncertainty expands.

Enforcing this speed limit assumes the permission path can demand the deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\) whenever safety requires it. Commanding rapid deceleration, however, requires sudden, large mechanical torque from the drive motors and gearboxes. If the permission path requests a torque step that exceeds what the actuator bandwidth budget allows, the mechanical body cannot deliver the required deceleration, invalidating the model-based stopping ceiling and risking collision. The same drives also shape what happens if the stop falls short. The reflected rotor energy counted in \(\EffectiveImpactMass\) then reaches the obstacle and the gear teeth through the transmission’s compliance, so the stopping calculation cannot substitute for a contact-load assessment.

2.4 Actuator Transmission Limits

When a policy writes a 50 N·m torque setpoint to a joint register or dispatches an emergency brake command over a Controller Area Network (CAN) bus or EtherCAT, the drive electronics may clamp the current at half that request while returning a clean acknowledgement packet in sub-millisecond time. In digital software, an execution target either accepts an instruction or raises a fault; in physical hardware, stator winding inductance, DC bus voltage headroom, magnetic saturation, and transmission elasticity bound what the joint actually delivers. The systems engineering challenge is to specify an actuator contract at the physical plant interface, written in delivered force, torque, slew-rate limits, response latency, and tracking error rather than digital setpoints, and to expose those limits directly to the runtime permission path before an unachievable command causes a collision.

2.4.1 Actuation mechanisms and the actuator contract

What that actuator contract must bound depends on how the joint converts stored energy into mechanical force. Electric drives dominate mobile manipulation and power the running machine of this book, where current in copper windings pulls on rotor magnets through a gear train. Other robots rely on pressurized fluid or calibrated mechanical springs, and each mechanism trades peak force density against bandwidth, compliance, and maintenance overhead.

On the sense-plan-act (S·P·A) triad (The Sense–Plan–Act Paradigm), the actuator contract sits at the Act node, where a command must become delivered force.

Across the broader field, three non-rigid or fluid alternatives illustrate how the physical medium reshapes the contract:

  • Hydraulic servovalves: For heavy machinery, construction robots, and high-load dynamic platforms, fluid power trades magnetic force for pressure. A cylinder’s force is supply pressure times piston area, \(\CylinderForce = \HydraulicSupplyPressure \PistonArea\), so raising the pressure lets a compact actuator deliver a large force. An electric motor’s force, by contrast, is bounded by the magnetic field its magnets and copper can sustain. The miniature servovalves on hydraulic legged robots respond fast, with bandwidths of hundreds of hertz (Scully 2015), so the valve is rarely the slow element. Hydraulic control is bounded instead by fluid compressibility (oil bulk modulus \(\OilBulkModulus\)), nonlinear orifice flow, and the plumbing, pumps, and cooling needed to keep the fluid within its operating range. That maintenance burden is one reason Boston Dynamics retired its hydraulic Atlas humanoid in 2024 in favor of an all-electric successor (Boston Dynamics 2024).
  • Pneumatics and soft actuation: Pneumatic artificial muscles and elastomeric chambers provide physical compliance that naturally cushions impacts. However, gas compressibility, valve flow restrictions, and acoustic transport delays limit closed-loop bandwidth to tens of hertz. Stored pneumatic energy can also discharge violently during rapid venting or line rupture. Safety validation requires qualifying the envelope of peak chamber pressure, flow rates, and measured contact dynamics.
  • Series elastic actuators (SEAs): By placing a calibrated spring directly between the gearbox and the output link (as in the ANYbotics ANYdrive), an SEA incorporates mechanical compliance into the drivetrain itself. The spring acts as a low-pass mechanical filter: it isolates delicate gear teeth from shock loads and decouples the large reflected rotor inertia (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\)) from the environment during impact. Its measured deflection also serves as a direct force sensor, since spring stiffness times deflection yields output torque (Pratt and Williamson 1995). Rather than dissipating energy, the spring stores elastic potential (\(\ElasticPotentialEnergy = \frac{1}{2} \SeriesElasticStiffness \FiniteChange\JointAngle^2\)) at the joint and can return that energy into an obstacle. The engineering trade-off is reduced closed-loop position bandwidth and low-frequency structural resonance. The safety system and permission path must therefore monitor physical spring deflection directly instead of assuming rigid load tracking.
Scully, Leah. 2015. Hydraulic Quadruped Uses Mini Servovalves. Power & Motion.
Boston Dynamics. 2024. An Electric New Era for Atlas. Boston Dynamics blog.

2.4.2 Electrical time constants, back-EMF, and current clamping

The running machine’s wheels and arm joints are electric, so the electric drive is the plant whose limits the stopping budget depends on. To a policy, a torque command is a number written to a register; to the motor, it is a current that must be built up in a coil. Three results from introductory physics describe that conversion (Lynch and Park 2017). First, a wire carrying current across a magnetic field feels a force proportional to the current. Wound around a rotor, those forces produce a torque proportional to current, \(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \WindingCurrent\), where the torque constant \(\MotorTorqueConstant\) (in \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m/A}\)) collects the magnet strength and the winding geometry. Second, the same wires, swept through the same field by the spinning rotor, have a voltage induced in them. A spinning motor is therefore also a generator, and its back-EMF (back electromotive force) \(\BackEmfVoltage = \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed\) opposes the supply. Energy conservation ties the two constants together. The electrical power the motor converts, \(\BackEmfVoltage\,\WindingCurrent\), equals the mechanical power it delivers, \(\MotorTorque \RotorAngularSpeed\), so \(\MotorBackEmfConstant\) and \(\MotorTorqueConstant\) are the same number in SI units. A three-phase drive’s current and voltage conventions scale them by constant factors, which is why both must be identified under one convention. Third, the winding is a coil, and a coil opposes any change in its current, \(\InductiveVoltage = \PhaseInductance\,d\WindingCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) for inductance \(\PhaseInductance\). Current, and therefore torque, cannot jump.

8 Field-Oriented Control (FOC): Holding the stator current at right angles to the rotor field is the arrangement that a brushed DC motor’s commutator enforces mechanically. An AC machine controlled this way behaves like a DC motor whose torque is set by a single current, \(\TorqueProducingCurrent\). That is what lets a policy command torque at all, because its request becomes a current target whose arrival the winding inductance and the voltage headroom then pace.

The budget needs two results from the motor controller: the torque delivered for a given current and the delay before that current appears. In a three-phase motor, field-oriented control (FOC) measures the winding currents and adjusts the inverter voltages so that the torque-producing current \(\TorqueProducingCurrent\) stays at right angles to the rotor’s magnetic field as the rotor turns. Torque is then \(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\).8 The controller splits the stator current into two components that turn with the rotor. The quadrature, or \(\Glyph{q}\)-axis, current \(\TorqueProducingCurrent\) lies at right angles to the rotor field and produces torque. The direct, or \(\Glyph{d}\)-axis, current \(\DirectAxisCurrent\) lies along the field, produces none, and is normally held at zero. The coordinate transformations inside the drive appear in section 2.4.6.

The delay between commanding a torque and delivering it begins in the stator windings. Adding the three effects around one winding circuit, the supply voltage must cover the resistive drop, the inductive voltage, and the back-EMF, which is Kirchhoff’s voltage law for the torque-producing current: \[\PhaseInductance \frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} + \PhaseResistance \TorqueProducingCurrent(\TimeInstant) = \QuadratureAxisVoltage(\TimeInstant) - \BackEmfVoltage(\TimeInstant)\] where \(\PhaseInductance\) is phase inductance, \(\PhaseResistance\) is phase resistance, \(\QuadratureAxisVoltage(\TimeInstant)\) is the applied quadrature voltage from the inverter bridge, \(\TorqueProducingCurrent(\TimeInstant)\) is the quadrature-axis (torque-producing) current, and \(\BackEmfVoltage(\TimeInstant) = \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed(\TimeInstant)\) is the back-EMF. The stator electrical time constant is: \[\ElectricalTimeConstant = \frac{\PhaseInductance}{\PhaseResistance}\]

If the inverter applied an uncontrolled voltage step, the current would approach its steady state exponentially along \(\TorqueProducingCurrent(\TimeInstant) = \SteadyStateCurrent(1 - e^{-\TimeInstant/\ElectricalTimeConstant})\), taking an entire time constant \(\ElectricalTimeConstant\) to reach 63 percent of the target. A closed-loop current controller drives the winding much faster. It applies full bus voltage until current reaches the setpoint, forcing a ramp rate of nearly \((\DcBusVoltage - \BackEmfVoltage)/\PhaseInductance\). At that point the current loop throttles the voltage to maintain regulation. On the running machine, this electrical rise is rapid. The hub motor windings (line inductance 1.885 mH, line resistance 0.7 Ω) yield \(\ElectricalTimeConstant =\) 2.7 ms. Starting from rest on a depleted 42 V pack, the initial current slope gives a resistance-free rise estimate of 1.1 ms, only 5.6 percent of the 20 ms brake onset budgeted for the platform. The resistive drop slows the ramp as current builds, so this estimate is optimistic and the full winding response must be measured. When braking from forward speed, the rotor continues spinning in the positive direction while the controller reverses the sign of the torque-producing current to oppose motion, so the positive back-EMF reinforces the inverter’s negative current drive, accelerates the current ramp further, and reduces the terminal voltage required to sustain peak braking current from \(\BackEmfVoltage + \InverterCurrentLimit \ElectricalResistance\) to \(\BackEmfVoltage - \InverterCurrentLimit \ElectricalResistance\). In this architecture, electrical rise time does not bottleneck emergency braking. Tire traction, maximum inverter current, and voltage headroom at high speed govern the stopping distance, as detailed in notebook 2.3.

Torque tracks current directly, which means available voltage headroom also dictates the maximum torque slew rate: \[\frac{\DerivativeOperator\MotorTorque}{d\TimeInstant} = \MotorTorqueConstant \frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} \le \frac{\MotorTorqueConstant (\DcBusVoltage - \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed)}{\PhaseInductance}\] Inductance and voltage conventions must align consistently: line-to-line values reported on manufacturer datasheets pair with the full DC bus voltage. Per-phase values modeled under space-vector modulation scale by an amplitude of approximately \(\DcBusVoltage/\sqrt{3}\), the modulation voltage limit stated in Actuator Electrical and Thermal Dynamics. At low wheel speeds, ample voltage headroom enables steep torque ramps. As rotational velocity climbs, back-EMF consumes that headroom, throttling the rate at which the inverter can inject current. An unconstrained policy setpoint requesting an instantaneous torque step is transformed by the motor hardware into a finite physical ramp; any demand exceeding this maximum slew rate produces severe tracking error. The safety stopping budget must account for this rate-limited response.

Voltage headroom bounds the rate of current rise; power electronics define the maximum current that can safely flow. Inverter bridges enforce a strict current ceiling \(\InverterCurrentLimit\) to protect the switching power transistors from thermal failure and prevent magnetic core saturation in the stator. For a motor coupled to a mechanical transmission with gear ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\) and mechanical efficiency \(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency\), this ceiling caps joint output torque at: \[\OutputTorqueCeiling = \GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio \MotorTorqueConstant \InverterCurrentLimit \tag{4}\]

This current limit settles a central safety requirement: whether the drives can sustain the deceleration demanded by the supervisory permission path. A motor drive may silently clamp current setpoints while continuing to acknowledge network commands; the safety path must interrogate measured output currents and status flags rather than inferring torque delivery from successful packet transmission. If emergency braking demands more torque than this ceiling allows, the assumed deceleration model breaks down. Any change to inverter current limits therefore invalidates the robot’s qualified stopping envelope and requires recomputing the stopping limit record (section 2.7).

Napkin Math 2.3: Can the drives deliver the credible deceleration?
The running machine brakes through two driven wheels, each a direct-drive 10-inch hub motor (the ZLTECH ZLLG10ASM800, with no gearbox, so \(\GearReductionRatio = 1\) and \(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \approx 1\)) (Shenzhen Zhongling Technology 2022) with rolling radius \(\WheelRadius =\) 133 mm. Its torque constant \(\MotorTorqueConstant =\) 2.4 N·m/A is the datasheet’s peak torque divided by its peak current, and the drive’s current limit is set at that peak, \(\InverterCurrentLimit =\) 25 A. Casters carry the rest of the weight.

Problem: Does the loaded machine’s credible deceleration \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s² fit under the traction, tip-over, and drive-torque ceilings, and does the drive keep control of its current at the 1.5 m/s drive limit?

Math:

  1. Traction: Only the driven wheels brake, so \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration \le \CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity \DrivenWheelLoadFraction\). With all the weight on them (\(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction = 1\)), the floor must supply \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \ge \CredibleBrakingDeceleration/\Gravity =\) 0.204. The hub motors are rated to carry 300 kg per pair, so on the 368 kg machine \(\DrivenWheelLoadFraction \le\) 0.815 and the floor must supply at least \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient =\) 0.250. The dry floor (\(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient =\) 0.60) meets that whenever the driven wheels carry at least a fraction 0.34 of the weight, and at the rated load share it supports \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient \Gravity \DrivenWheelLoadFraction =\) 4.80 m/s². The inspected floor, a sealed low-traction surface whose friction a facility inspection certifies only down to \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient =\) 0.12, supports only 1.18 m/s² even with every kilogram on the driven wheels.
  2. Tip-over: The machine stays on its wheels while \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration \le \Gravity\,\LeadingSupportDistance/\GravityCenterHeight\), so the credible deceleration requires \(\GravityCenterHeight/\LeadingSupportDistance \le \Gravity/\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 4.90. The machine’s specification gives neither the loaded center-of-gravity height nor its distance to the leading support, and extending the arm forward shortens \(\LeadingSupportDistance\). This ceiling cannot yet be checked.
  3. Drive torque: Each wheel delivers \(\MotorTorqueConstant \InverterCurrentLimit =\) 60 N·m, so the floor sees \(\DriveBrakingForceCeiling = 2 \MotorTorqueConstant \InverterCurrentLimit/\WheelRadius =\) 902.3 N. With the rotating-mass bound of section 2.3.1, \(\EffectiveImpactMass \le\) 387 kg, and the torque ceiling is \(\DriveBrakingForceCeiling/\EffectiveImpactMass \ge\) 2.33 m/s², at least 16.6 percent above the credible deceleration. With the current limit set at the rated 8.5 A instead, each wheel gives its rated 20 N·m and the ceiling falls to 0.78 m/s².
  4. Voltage at the drive limit: At 1.5 m/s the wheel turns at \(\RotorAngularSpeed = \SpeedBound/\WheelRadius =\) 107.7 rpm, and the line-to-line back-EMF is \(\BackEmfVoltage = \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed =\) 28.2 V. Braking current flows with the back-EMF, so holding \(\InverterCurrentLimit\) through the 0.7 Ω line resistance requires only \(\BackEmfVoltage - \InverterCurrentLimit \ElectricalResistance =\) 10.7 V at the terminals. Even a depleted 42 V pack sits 13.8 V above \(\BackEmfVoltage\). Driving at the same current would need \(\BackEmfVoltage + \InverterCurrentLimit \ElectricalResistance =\) 45.7 V, more than the depleted pack supplies.

Result: The drives clear 2 m/s² by at least 16.6 percent, but only with the current limit at the motor’s peak. Traction holds on the dry floor if the driven wheels carry at least a fraction 0.34 of the weight, and it fails on the inspected floor. The tip-over ceiling remains unverified until the loaded center of gravity is measured, by weighing each wheel on corner scales or by a tilt test, with the arm stowed and extended.

Systems insight: The credible deceleration is a conjunction of three ceilings owned by different parts of the machine: the floor, the mass distribution that the arm changes, and the drive configuration. A current limit lowered to protect the windings cuts the torque ceiling below the stopping premise without any software change, so the configured current limit belongs in the limit record’s conditions.

Shenzhen Zhongling Technology. 2022. ZLLG10ASM800 Robot Hub Motor: Motor Parameters. Product drawing and datasheet.

A 2 m/s² stop must clear every ceiling; two fall short and one is unmeasured.

9 Inverter Dead Time: A short hardware-inserted interval in which both switches of an inverter half-bridge are off, so that the high-side and low-side switches of one leg never conduct together and short the DC bus, a failure called shoot-through. The interval distorts the applied voltage most at low speed and near each current zero crossing, so delivered torque departs from the request exactly where slow, fine contact force is commanded.

Current regulation also incurs electrical and switching losses. Any stator current vector misaligned with the quadrature axis produces resistive heating without contributing shaft torque. Inverter pulse-width modulation and required protective dead times introduce further nonlinear voltage distortion.9 Consequently, dynamic torque tracking must be verified through physical dynamometer measurements on the assembled actuator rather than inferred from current setpoints alone.

2.4.3 Reflected rotor inertia and optimal inertia matching

Once electromagnetic torque leaves the motor shaft, mechanical gearing introduces a dynamic asymmetry between torque and inertia. High-reduction joints employ gear ratios between \(50{:}1\) and \(160{:}1\) to multiply the output torque of compact, lightweight motors. While this reduces the motor torque required by a factor of \(\GearReductionRatio\), the motor rotor must spin \(\GearReductionRatio\) times faster than the actuated joint. When an external contact backdrives the joint, it must accelerate that rotor at \(\GearReductionRatio\) times the joint’s angular acceleration. Because rotational kinetic energy scales with the square of velocity (\(\frac{1}{2} \RotorInertia \RotorAngularSpeed^2\)), the rotor’s physical inertia \(\RotorInertia\) resists load-side acceleration multiplied by the square of the gear ratio (definition 2.3).

Through a 100:1 transmission, a small 50 g rotor imposes the apparent inertia of a heavy 11.2 kg flywheel with a 10 cm radius at the output shaft (Lynch and Park 2017). When the end-effector collides with a rigid surface, the contact force must instantly decelerate this equivalent flywheel through the drivetrain, generating extreme peak stresses that can fracture gear teeth at their roots, as documented in figure 4.

Definition 2.3: Reflected rotor inertia

Reflected rotor inertia (\(\ReflectedRotorInertia\)) is the apparent rotational inertia of an actuator rotor at the load-side joint output, scaling with the square of the transmission gear ratio: \[\ReflectedRotorInertia = \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\]

  1. Significance: In high-reduction transmissions (\(\GearReductionRatio \ge 100\)) it dominates the apparent load, capping joint acceleration \(\JointAcceleration\) and setting impact shock loads.
  2. Distinction: Unlike rotor inertia \(\RotorInertia\) or link inertia \(\LoadInertia\), it acts through transmission stiffness and backlash, so backdriving from the environment meets an \(\GearReductionRatio^2\) barrier.
  3. Common pitfall: Ignoring reflected rotor inertia during collision and contact modeling. Under sudden obstacle impact, the reflected kinetic energy of the spinning rotor concentrates stress directly on gear teeth before drive current can be interrupted.

The combined equation of motion for a geared joint driving load inertia \(\LoadInertia\) with motor torque \(\MotorTorque\) is: \[ \JointAcceleration = \frac{\GearReductionRatio \MotorTorque}{\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia} \] Maximizing delivered load acceleration \(\JointAcceleration\) with respect to transmission ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\), by setting \(\DerivativeOperator\JointAcceleration/d\GearReductionRatio = 0\), gives \(\LoadInertia = \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\): acceleration peaks when the reflected rotor inertia equals the load inertia. Solving for \(\GearReductionRatio\) establishes the classical inertia matching ratio:10 \[ \InertiaMatchingRatio = \sqrt{\frac{\LoadInertia}{\RotorInertia}} \tag{5}\]

10 Inertia Matching: At \(\InertiaMatchingRatio\) the reflected rotor inertia equals the load inertia, the mechanical twin of impedance matching in a circuit, where a source delivers the most power to a load whose impedance equals its own. The analogy carries the same caveat: the matched ratio maximizes acceleration, not efficiency or safety, so practical arms choose lower ratios to protect gear teeth from shock, keep the joint backdrivable, and raise top speed.

Below \(\InertiaMatchingRatio\), joint acceleration is torque-limited: increasing \(\GearReductionRatio\) multiplies motor torque to accelerate the heavy load faster. Above \(\InertiaMatchingRatio\), more gearing decreases delivered joint acceleration, because the motor expends most of its electromagnetic torque accelerating its own spinning rotor (\(\GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\)) rather than the external link. For the 50 g rotor above driving an illustrative link of \(\LoadInertia =\) 8.5 × 10⁻³ kg·m², the matched ratio is \(\InertiaMatchingRatio \approx\) 39. At 100:1 the same motor torque delivers only 67.7 percent of the matched-ratio acceleration, because \(\JointAcceleration(\GearReductionRatio)/\JointAcceleration(\InertiaMatchingRatio) = 2\NormalizedGearRatio/(1+\NormalizedGearRatio^2)\) with \(\NormalizedGearRatio = \GearReductionRatio/\InertiaMatchingRatio\). The extra gearing has cost 32.3 percent of the joint’s acceleration.

The gear ratio also magnifies the mechanical resistance an external contact meets when attempting to move the link. This resistance to imposed motion constitutes the joint’s mechanical impedance. Because link inertia, joint damping, torsional compliance, and seal friction all shape this response, the gear ratio alone does not establish contact forces or structural fracture limits. Transmission selection must balance delivered joint acceleration against the impedance presented to accidental impacts.

Past \(\InertiaMatchingRatio \approx\) 39, gearing chokes acceleration; 100:1 keeps 67.7 percent.

Abrupt changes in commanded torque excite the torsional elasticity of geared joints before the physical link responds. Bounding joint acceleration and jerk moderates this excitation, but software trajectory smoothing cannot override mechanical resonance. Trajectory generators formulate control profiles that respect these physical boundaries (Lynch and Park 2017).
