The hardware platform must provide the empirical response rates and contact stiffness limits required to tune them.

↪ Trajectory timing under joint torque and jerk bounds is developed in Time parameterization under torque and jerk bounds.

2.4.4 Transmission compliance, contact impedance, and tooth fracture

Mechanical gearboxes introduce backlash and elasticity that decouple the motor from the driven link. Upon direction reversal, the motor shaft traverses mechanical clearance before the gear teeth engage the opposing flank. Concurrently, as illustrated by the disassembled strain wave gear set in figure 3, torsional compliance in gear teeth and flexible elements11 transforms the rigid link into an elastic system (Spong 1987). Its dynamics follow the flexible-joint formulation given in Flexible joint dynamics and singular perturbations. Step changes in torque excite this structural spring, driving the drivetrain into limit-cycle12 chatter. Motor-side encoders conceal this deadband completely. The motor rotates smoothly while the output shaft remains pinned, causing link position \(\LinkOutputPosition\) to diverge from \(\MotorShaftPosition / \GearReductionRatio\) by a load-dependent deflection \(\LostMotionDeflection\). The motor controller observes flawless setpoint tracking while the end-effector stalls, eventually experiencing an impulsive impact when the gear teeth make contact. Repeated shock cycles culminate in the root fatigue failure shown in figure 4.

11 Strain Wave Gearing: Musser (1959) patented the mechanism, now sold as the Harmonic Drive: an elliptical wave generator flexes a thin toothed cup inside a rigid ring that carries two more teeth, so a single stage yields a high ratio with near-zero backlash. The flexing that makes the ratio possible is also the joint’s torsional compliance, which makes the link elastic.

Musser, C. Walton. 1959. Strain Wave Gearing. U.S. Patent 2,906,143, filed March 21, 1955, issued September 29, 1959.

12 Limit Cycle: A self-sustained oscillation of fixed amplitude that a nonlinear feedback loop settles into, here excited by the backlash deadband as the motor crosses the gap, the teeth strike, and the controller corrects back across it. Unlike an instability, it does not grow, so it can run unnoticed through a shift while every cycle adds a tooth impact to the fatigue count.

The strain wave gear of figure 3 packs a high reduction into a compact joint by deforming its flexspline continuously, and that elasticity has a cost. This intentional compliance decouples rotor position from link position under dynamic loading: while the motor encoder registers smooth rotation, the flexspline winds up like a torsion spring, introducing unmeasured end-effector deflection.

A tooth broken off at its root (figure 4) is the end state of that loading. Such failures show why dynamic impact loads, rather than static payload ratings alone, must govern actuator and transmission selection: repeated torque spikes and unmodeled contact shocks concentrate destructive stresses directly at the tooth root fillet. While smooth motion plans mitigate drivetrain vibration, safe physical interaction depends on measuring output torque and enforcing strict mechanical load boundaries.

Systems Perspective 2.3: Impedance control for contact safety
When a position-controlled robot encounters a rigid surface (contact stiffness \(\ContactStiffness \ge 10^5\text{ N/m}\)), a minor setpoint tracking error of \(\FiniteChange \ContactPointPosition = 1\text{ mm}\) escalates the contact force to \(\ContactForce = \ContactStiffness \FiniteChange \ContactPointPosition = 100\text{ N}\) within milliseconds as the inverter commands maximum current against the obstacle. On the mobile manipulator, the gripper contacting the spring-latched door strikes a steel plate with stiffness 4.0 × 10⁵ N/m. Once impact occurs, contact force ramps at \(\ContactForce = \ContactStiffness \ContactApproachSpeed \TimeInstant\): 12 N/ms under a guarded approach velocity of 0.03 m/s, and 40 N/ms at an unconstrained 0.10 m/s, rapidly exceeding the latch’s 100 N structural limit.

Impedance control (Hogan 1985) reformulates contact interactions away from pure position or force control. An impedance takes motion in and returns force; an admittance takes force in and returns motion. Because a rigid environment behaves as an admittance, yielding motion only when pushed, the manipulator is made to behave as an impedance, which answers a deviation in position and velocity with a restoring force (\(\ContactForce = \MechanicalImpedanceMap(\ContactDirectionVelocity)\), force as a function of the imposed motion). The joint drive controller realizes this relationship by emulating a virtual spring-damper system: \[\ContactForce = \PidProportionalGain (\DesiredContactPosition - \ContactPointPosition) + \PidDerivativeGain (\DesiredContactVelocity - \ContactDirectionVelocity)\] The stiffness \(\PidProportionalGain\) multiplies the position error and the damping \(\PidDerivativeGain\) the velocity error, the roles a proportional and a derivative gain play in a feedback controller. Tuning them in software causes the arm to yield compliantly under tracking errors, shedding peak impact loads. However, closed-loop impedance behavior is physically bounded by current-loop update rates, inverter voltage headroom, and unmodeled joint elasticity. Algebraic control laws cannot alter the unyielding inertia of the rotor during the first milliseconds of an impact. The runtime permission path must therefore enforce approach speed limits derived from physical contact qualification rather than relying on software compliance alone. The impedance command interface, through which a learned policy proposes these gains and the permission path validates them before admission, is defined in What a proposal may command.

2.4.5 Actuation regimes and gear reduction trade-offs

The scaling disparity underlying inertia matching, in which output torque scales linearly with ratio (\(\JointOutputTorque = \GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio \MotorTorque\)) while reflected inertia scales quadratically (\(\GearReductionRatio^2\)), divides robotic actuation into three primary regimes. These regimes trade continuous torque density against mechanical backdrivability, distinguished by gear design, stage count, and reduction ratio (table 2): at the lowest ratios, quasi-direct drive (QDD) pairs a high-torque motor with a single compact planetary stage; at intermediate ratios, multistage planetary gearboxes reduce motor size at the cost of moderate reflected inertia and backlash; and at the highest ratios, strain-wave gears pack large torque multiplication into a compact joint at the cost of extreme reflected inertia and torsional compliance.

Actuator selection must satisfy continuous torque targets and permissible contact dynamics simultaneously. Direct-drive configurations eliminate gear backlash entirely, but require large, heavy motors to produce holding torque (Asada and Kanade 1983). Increasing the gear ratio permits a much smaller motor to produce equivalent output torque, but introduces reflected inertia, friction, and transmission compliance.

Motor thermal limits and torque requirements intersect in an electromagnetic figure of merit. Stator resistive losses follow \(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\), where \(\EffectiveLossResistance\) is the winding’s effective loss resistance, while electromagnetic torque follows \(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\). Combining these expressions reveals that resistive dissipation scales with the square of motor torque: \(\WindingLossPower = (\MotorTorque / \MotorConstant)^2\). Here the motor constant \(\MotorConstant = \MotorTorqueConstant / \sqrt{\EffectiveLossResistance}\) (in \(\text{N}\cdot\text{m}/\sqrt{\text{W}}\)) captures the motor’s torque-producing capacity per square root of dissipated heat. Motor datasheets specify this parameter directly (TQ-Systems 2014). Section 2.5.1 relates \(\EffectiveLossResistance\) to the phase and line-to-line resistances of section 2.4.2. When driving a mechanical transmission of ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\) and efficiency \(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency\), the motor must supply an internal torque of \(\JointOutputTorque/(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio)\), resulting in: \[\WindingLossPower = \left(\frac{\JointOutputTorque}{\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio \MotorConstant}\right)^2, \qquad \ReflectedTransmissionInputInertia = \GearReductionRatio^2 \TransmissionInputInertia\] where \(\TransmissionInputInertia\) represents total input inertia (the rotor plus the transmission input stage), and \(\ReflectedTransmissionInputInertia\) is that combined inertia reflected to the joint output. Increasing the reduction ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\) reduces winding heat by \(\GearReductionRatio^2\) while multiplying reflected inertia by \(\GearReductionRatio^2\). Consequently, for a selected motor and gearbox geometry, their product is invariant to the gear ratio: \[\WindingLossPower\,\ReflectedTransmissionInputInertia = \frac{\JointOutputTorque^2\,\TransmissionInputInertia}{\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency^2 \MotorConstant^2} \tag{6}\] Adjusting \(\GearReductionRatio\) relocates the actuator along a fixed trade-off curve between thermal dissipation and reflected inertia. Improving both characteristics concurrently requires selecting a motor with a superior ratio of \(\MotorConstant^2/\TransmissionInputInertia\). Notebook 2.4 evaluates this trade-off for the mobile manipulator’s arm joints.

Napkin Math 2.4: What does the gear ratio cost?
The running machine’s arm is a seven-joint, torque-sensing manipulator in the class of the Franka Emika Panda (Franka Emika GmbH 2018), whose joints use strain-wave gears (The machine data sheet). Franka does not publish the motors in those joints, so this example, like the thermal examples of section 2.5, uses a sourced stand-in. The stand-in is a TQ-RoboDrive ILM 70x18 frameless motor (TQ-Systems 2014) with \(\MotorTorqueConstant =\) 0.187 N·m/A and effective loss resistance \(\EffectiveLossResistance =\) 0.495 Ω, driving a CSG-25 strain-wave gear (Harmonic Drive AG 2014), whose catalog lists one input inertia for all of that size’s ratios. Rotor plus gear input give \(\TransmissionInputInertia =\) 7.34 × 10⁻⁵ kg·m². The joint must hold the arm’s maximum joint torque \(\JointOutputTorque =\) 87 N·m through a gear of efficiency \(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency =\) 0.85 (illustrative).

Problem: How do the copper loss and the reflected inertia change if the same motor drives the joint through 50:1, 100:1, or a quasi-direct-drive ratio of 10:1?

Math:

  1. Motor constant: \(\MotorConstant = \MotorTorqueConstant/\sqrt{\EffectiveLossResistance} =\) 0.266 N·m/√W, the value the motor’s data sheet publishes.
  2. At 50:1: The motor supplies \(\JointOutputTorque/(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio) =\) 2.05 N·m, dissipating \((\MotorTorque/\MotorConstant)^2 =\) 59.3 W, and the joint reflects \(\GearReductionRatio^2 \TransmissionInputInertia =\) 0.183 kg·m².
  3. At 100:1: The motor supplies 1.02 N·m, the loss falls fourfold to 14.8 W, and the reflected inertia rises fourfold to 0.734 kg·m². The product \(\WindingLossPower \ReflectedTransmissionInputInertia\) is unchanged.
  4. At 10:1: The motor would have to supply 10.2 N·m, 2.5× its 4.05 N·m peak torque. No current can make this motor hold the load.

Result: Doubling the ratio buys a fourfold cut in holding heat with a fourfold rise in the inertia that an impact must accelerate, and lowering the ratio toward quasi-direct drive fails outright with this motor. The gear ratio moves the joint along a fixed trade; only the motor sets the trade itself.

Systems insight: A low-ratio, backdrivable joint requires a motor with a much larger torque per square root of watt, the route quasi-direct drive takes. A high-ratio arm joint instead accepts the reflected inertia and recovers contact sensitivity by measuring torque at its output.

Mechanical backdrivability measures the ease with which an external load applied at the joint output rotates the motor shaft backward through the transmission. The external torque required to backdrive a geared joint is: \[\ExternalJointTorque = \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia \JointAcceleration + \JointFrictionTorque\] where \(\JointFrictionTorque\) denotes total joint friction referred to the output link. This friction varies dynamically with operating state (Lynch and Park 2017): breakaway static friction dominates at rest, Coulomb friction opposes motion at constant levels, and viscous damping grows with velocity. The Stribeck effect, a dip in friction just after sliding begins, shapes the response at near-zero speeds. Furthermore, gear tooth mesh friction scales with transmitted load, ambient temperature, and lubrication aging. At low velocities, friction dominates the resistance of high-reduction gearboxes; during sudden impacts, reflected rotor inertia dominates. As a result, backdrivability must be verified through empirical load testing on the assembled joint rather than inferred from the reduction ratio alone.

Quasi-direct drive actuation takes the low-ratio route of notebook 2.4, using large-diameter, high-torque motors so a single modest reduction stage suffices (Seok et al. 2015; Wensing et al. 2017; Katz et al. 2019). Minimizing reflected inertia and internal friction maximizes mechanical transparency, the fidelity with which external interaction forces translate into measurable motor motion and winding currents. The penalty is a substantially heavier motor demand for any given output torque.

Katz, Benjamin, Jared Di Carlo, and Sangbae Kim. 2019. “Mini Cheetah: A Platform for Dynamic Locomotion.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 3295–301. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICRA.2019.8793865.

Mechanical transparency also dictates what motor currents reveal about contact states. In high-friction gearboxes, motor current primarily overcomes internal transmission losses rather than external loads. Conversely, when transmission friction and inertial resistance remain small, output torque simplifies to: \[\JointOutputTorque \approx \GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\] This relationship enables proprioceptive sensing, allowing the controller to estimate interaction forces directly from motor current without specialized external sensors. However, accurate estimation requires rigorous calibration across operating temperatures and speeds; current setpoints measure motor electromagnetic effort, portions of which are absorbed by acceleration and drivetrain losses. While backdrivability allows external collisions to displace the joint without shearing gear teeth, low gearing and motor current sensing do not guarantee safe contact forces. High link velocities can still impart severe impulsive shock before the current controller can respond. Safe interaction mandates qualifying the total closed-loop contact response.

High-reduction joints overcome transmission friction by sensing torque directly at the joint output. Strain gauge torque sensors mounted on the output side of strain wave gearboxes measure delivered torque directly, allowing closed-loop current controllers to cancel gearbox friction and cogging torque (Lynch and Park 2017). The running machine’s arm adopts this approach, integrating torque sensors within each of its seven joints (Franka Emika GmbH 2018) to achieve transparency through measurement rather than low mechanical reduction. The engineering cost includes increased joint complexity, elevated mass, and the torsional compliance of the strain wave flexsplines, which complicates high-bandwidth motion tracking.

Franka Emika GmbH. 2018. Panda Datasheet. Product datasheet (reseller-hosted copy).

Whether mechanical transparency originates by construction (quasi-direct drive) or by active measurement (output torque sensing), contact operations require qualifying joint compliance, closed-loop torque response, and dynamic sensing fidelity against the operating environment. Dual-encoder architectures and load-side torque sensors expose internal transmission windup and mechanical backlash that motor-side encoders conceal. When load-side deflection departs from the qualified dynamic envelope, the runtime safety system can immediately intercede and reject subsequent motion commands.

2.4.6 Supporting drive and impedance models

Deliverable motor torque and contact shock isolation rest on two compact models of how the drive and transmission transform effort and motion: the rotating-frame torque law behind \(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\) (section 2.4.2), and the series-spring impedance that isolates reflected rotor inertia during a fast impact (section 2.4.4). Inside the physical drive, FOC holds the \(\Glyph{d}\)-axis current \(\DirectAxisCurrent\) of section 2.4.2 at zero, which minimizes stator losses and makes output torque proportional to the \(\Glyph{q}\)-axis current alone (Actuator Electrical and Thermal Dynamics states the voltage equations that govern both currents in the rotating frame): \[\MotorTorque = \frac{3}{2} \RotorPolePairCount \PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage \TorqueProducingCurrent = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\] where \(\RotorPolePairCount\) is the number of magnetic pole pairs on the rotor and \(\PermanentMagnetFluxLinkage\) is the permanent-magnet flux linkage, the magnetic flux from the rotor magnets that threads each winding. The \(\frac{3}{2}\) scaling factor arises from the amplitude-invariant transformation convention adopted in this chapter. Motor drive firmware must enforce a transformation convention consistent with the identified torque constant \(\MotorTorqueConstant\).

For frequency-domain dynamic analysis, mechanical impedance quantifies the joint’s physical resistance to imposed environmental motion. The Laplace variable \(\LaplaceVariable\) stands for differentiation in time, so that for a sinusoidal motion of angular frequency \(\AngularFrequency\) it can be read as \(\LaplaceVariable = \ImaginaryUnit\AngularFrequency\). An impedance term \(\LaplaceVariable \RotatingBodyInertia\) grows with frequency like an inertia, and a term \(\RotationalSpringStiffness/\LaplaceVariable\) falls with frequency like a spring. The apparent mechanical impedance \(\JointOutputImpedance(\LaplaceVariable) = \ExternalJointTorque(\LaplaceVariable) / \JointVelocity(\LaplaceVariable)\) evaluated at the joint output flange couples the load dynamics directly to the reflected rotor dynamics through the drivetrain’s torsional stiffness \(\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness\). An external torque applied at the joint flange splits between two paths that share the same output velocity \(\JointVelocity(\LaplaceVariable)\), one accelerating the output link inertia \(\LoadInertia\) directly and the other twisting the transmission spring against the reflected rotor. Along that second path, the transmission spring and the reflected rotor act in series so both stages bear equal transmitted torque while their relative angular velocities sum, causing the spring impedance \(\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness/\LaplaceVariable\) and reflected rotor impedance to combine through their reciprocal sum before adding to the output link impedance (Spong 1987): \[\JointOutputImpedance(\LaplaceVariable) = \LaplaceVariable \LoadInertia + \OutputBearingDamping + \left[\frac{1}{\LaplaceVariable \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \MotorShaftDamping} + \frac{\LaplaceVariable}{\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness}\right]^{-1}\] The coefficients \(\OutputBearingDamping\) and \(\MotorShaftDamping\) account for viscous damping in the output bearings and motor shaft. At low interaction frequencies, the series spring behaves rigidly relative to the rotor, and the environment experiences the aggregate inertia \(\LoadInertia + \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\) assumed in rigid-body kinematic models. Above the transmission’s natural resonance frequency, the bracketed impedance term simplifies toward \(\TransmissionTorsionalStiffness/\LaplaceVariable\): external collision forces compress the joint compliance against the link inertia \(\LoadInertia\), temporarily isolating the rotor’s reflected inertia. This series compliance cushions sudden impacts in flexible drivetrains and series elastic actuators, reinforcing why the flexible-joint dynamic model is necessary to calculate contact loads during manipulation.

Checkpoint 2.1: Actuator limits

The drives decide whether the stopping premise can be delivered, and the transmission decides what that delivery costs in heat and inertia.

Operating near these electrical and mechanical ceilings exacts an inevitable thermodynamic toll. Every ampere driving the motor against reflected rotor inertia or sustaining peak clamping torque dissipates resistive heat (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\)) in the stator windings. That thermal energy accumulates directly in the motor’s thermal mass, bounding the operational duration of peak torque before winding insulation suffers accelerated thermal degradation.

2.5 Thermal Duty Cycles

Holding a load motionless at full reach places deceptive demands on an articulated arm such as the mobile manipulator’s. Maintaining a static load against gravity demands continuous phase current through the stator coils, actively converting electrical energy into accumulating heat. Heat arrives in seconds and leaves in minutes, making duty cycle a primary design variable.

2.5.1 Thermal capacity and insulation breakdown thresholds

Although a stationary joint performs zero mechanical work (\(\MechanicalWork = \int \AppliedForce \cdot d\BodyDisplacement = 0\)), sustaining its holding torque against gravity requires continuous phase current to produce magnetic Lorentz force (\(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\)). The lumped thermal model uses \(\TorqueProducingCurrent\) in the same convention as its torque constant \(\MotorTorqueConstant\). Here \(\EffectiveLossResistance\) denotes the effective aggregate loss resistance for that current, so total winding copper loss is: \[\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance = \frac{\MotorTorque^2}{\MotorTorqueConstant^2} \EffectiveLossResistance\] For the amplitude-invariant drive convention above, with balanced phase currents and \(\DirectAxisCurrent=0\), this effective resistance is \(\EffectiveLossResistance=\frac{3}{2}\PhaseResistance\), where \(\PhaseResistance\) is resistance per phase. Three resistances of one winding therefore appear in this chapter. The phase resistance \(\PhaseResistance\) belongs in the Kirchhoff equation of section 2.4.2. Datasheets quote the line-to-line value measured between two terminals, which for a star-connected winding spans two phases, \(2\PhaseResistance\). The hub-motor current-rise example used that line value with the line inductance, whose ratio gives the same time constant. The thermal calculations use \(\EffectiveLossResistance = \frac{3}{2}\PhaseResistance\), three quarters of the line-to-line value, and every winding resistance in them, including \(\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature)\), is this effective resistance. The following illustrative thermal examples stipulate it and the matching current convention.

The copper-loss equation ties torque to current and current to heat. Commanded torque scales linearly with current (\(\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\)), but stator copper loss scales quadratically with torque (\(\MotorTorque^2\)). Demanding twice the torque requires four times the thermal dissipation. Because heat has no fast exit, thermal accumulation limits how long an arm can maintain static poses or execute dynamic bursts without mechanical brakes.

Reducing motor current to shed heat also reduces holding torque. A loaded arm therefore needs a response that preserves support, such as transferring the load to a mechanical brake, rather than assuming that thermal derating leaves the task unchanged. Excess winding temperature can damage electrical insulation, while excess rotor temperature can permanently reduce magnet strength. Those material limits bound the allowable temperature trajectory.

The winding and housing respond on different timescales. Copper heats rapidly under current, while the larger core and housing absorb and reject heat more slowly. A case temperature measurement can therefore lag the winding hotspot during a torque burst. The stator in figure 5 failed from overheating, and its damage sits where this model puts the heat: in the copper windings and the insulation between them.

A thermal model follows the same energy accounting as the stopping calculation: energy accumulates where the rate entering exceeds the rate leaving. Here the winding receives electrical losses and transfers heat to the core; the core transfers heat to the surroundings. Thermal capacitance measures how much energy raises a component’s temperature, while thermal resistance measures how strongly the heat-flow path resists that transfer. Representing the winding and core separately gives the two-node lumped-parameter thermal ordinary differential equation (ODE) system: \[\begin{aligned} \WindingThermalCapacitance \frac{d\WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} &= \WindingLossPower(\TimeInstant) - \frac{\WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant) - \StatorCoreTemperature(\TimeInstant)}{\WindingCoreThermalResistance} \\ \CoreThermalCapacitance \frac{d\StatorCoreTemperature(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} &= \frac{\WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant) - \StatorCoreTemperature(\TimeInstant)}{\WindingCoreThermalResistance} - \frac{\StatorCoreTemperature(\TimeInstant) - \AmbientTemperature}{\CoreAmbientThermalResistance} \end{aligned}\] where \(\WindingLossPower(\TimeInstant) = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2(\TimeInstant) \ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature) = \frac{\MotorTorque^2(\TimeInstant)}{\MotorTorqueConstant^2} \ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature)\) is the Joule dissipation source deposited directly into the copper coils, \(\WindingThermalCapacitance\) is the small thermal capacitance of the winding, \(\CoreThermalCapacitance\) is the large thermal capacitance of the laminated stator core and housing, \(\WindingCoreThermalResistance\) is the conductive thermal resistance from winding to core, and \(\CoreAmbientThermalResistance\) is the convective thermal resistance from core to ambient air.

This separation of timescales creates a dangerous observability gap. Because \(\WindingThermalCapacitance \ll \CoreThermalCapacitance\), under a sudden peak torque burst or sustained stall against gravity, the stator core temperature \(\StatorCoreTemperature\) remains virtually clamped over the initial seconds by its large thermal inertia. The internal copper winding temperature surges at the initial adiabatic rate: \[\left.\frac{d\WindingTemperature}{d\TimeInstant}\right|_{t=0} \approx \frac{\WindingLossPower}{\WindingThermalCapacitance}\] Under sustained peak torque, the internal copper hotspot \(\WindingTemperature\) can climb past the 155 °C Class F rating within tens of seconds (notebook 2.5) while an external thermistor bonded to the motor casing still reads far below the hotspot. The class temperature is not a cliff at which the insulation fails. IEC 60085 assigns it as the hot-spot temperature at which an insulation system reaches its rated life (International Electrotechnical Commission 2007), and insulation aging13 proceeds as a chemical reaction whose rate grows exponentially with temperature (Dakin 1948). Every excursion above the rating therefore spends insulation life faster than the rating assumes, and the accumulated aging culminates in the insulation charring and turn-to-turn electrical failures documented in figure 5. A thermal budget must count accumulated exposure as well as the instantaneous peak.

13 Thermal Aging as an Exchange Rate: The engineering practice predates the chemistry. Montsinger (1930) proposed loading transformers by the temperature their insulation reaches rather than by current alone, and Dakin (1948) later explained the exponential dependence as Arrhenius reaction kinetics. The consequence is an exchange rate rather than a threshold. Time spent above the rating costs life at a rate that grows exponentially with the excess, so the limit record must count time at temperature rather than merely the peak.

Montsinger, V. M. 1930. “Loading Transformers by Temperature.” Transactions of the American Institute of Electrical Engineers 49 (2): 776–90. https://doi.org/10.1109/T-AIEE.1930.5055572.
Dakin, Thomas W. 1948. “Electrical Insulation Deterioration Treated as a Chemical Rate Phenomenon.” Transactions of the American Institute of Electrical Engineers 67 (1): 113–22. https://doi.org/10.1109/T-AIEE.1948.5059649.

A first-order approximation can summarize heating over a characterized operating range, with an effective winding temperature \(\WindingTemperature\) and fitted thermal parameters: \[\StatorThermalCapacitance \frac{d\WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant)}{d\TimeInstant} = \WindingLossPower(\TimeInstant) - \frac{\WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant) - \AmbientTemperature}{\ThermalResistance}\] where \(\ThermalResistance = \WindingCoreThermalResistance + \CoreAmbientThermalResistance\) represents the winding-to-ambient steady-state thermal resistance. The effective capacitance \(\StatorThermalCapacitance\) sets the fitted time constant \(\ThermalTimeConstant = \ThermalResistance \StatorThermalCapacitance\). This approximation does not resolve a fast winding hotspot separately from the housing. Its transient predictions require validation before they can bound a burst.

At thermal steady state (\(d\WindingTemperature/d\TimeInstant = 0\)), heat generation balances heat dissipation to ambient: \[\SustainableCopperLossPower =\frac{\WindingTemperatureLimit - \AmbientTemperature}{\ThermalResistance}\] Substituting the copper loss identity \(\WindingLossPower = \frac{\MotorTorque^2}{\MotorTorqueConstant^2} \ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperatureLimit)\) reveals the continuous thermal torque ceiling: \[\ContinuousTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \sqrt{\frac{\WindingTemperatureLimit - \AmbientTemperature}{\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperatureLimit) \ThermalResistance}} \tag{7}\] Continuous torque is limited by thermal dissipation, not by the peak current ratings of the drive electronics or the battery. The arm’s stand-in motor, for example, is rated for 4.05 N·m peak but only 1.25 N·m continuous, a ratio of 3.2 (TQ-Systems 2014). A brief torque burst of several seconds causes a rapid transient temperature rise that stays within the insulation rating (\(\WindingTemperatureLimit =\) 155 °C) if its peak does. However, sustained holding torque or high-frequency cycling drives the winding toward an asymptotic thermal equilibrium \(\EquilibriumWindingTemperature = \AmbientTemperature + \AverageCopperLossPower \ThermalResistance\). If that equilibrium exceeds the rating, continuous operation ages the winding insulation far faster than its rated life allows and ends in phase-to-phase short circuits.

The loss term must also change with temperature. Copper’s positive temperature coefficient (\(\CopperTemperatureCoefficient \approx 0.00393\text{ K}^{-1}\)) increases the effective winding resistance by 47.2 percent from 20 °C to 140 °C, according to \(\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature) = \ReferenceWindingResistance [1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient(\WindingTemperature - \CopperReferenceTemperature)]\). That upper temperature is the engineering ceiling \(\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling\), a working limit chosen below the 155 °C insulation rating \(\WindingTemperatureLimit\) to leave room for the error of the thermal model. The burst horizons and the stall derating below are checked against the rating itself. A schedule of repeated bursts is held to the ceiling, as is the installed continuous rating that the datasheet pitfall of section 2.9 obtains by evaluating equation 7 at \(\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling\) in place of \(\WindingTemperatureLimit\). Holding the same torque at the ceiling therefore dissipates 47.2 percent more winding power than at the reference. A fixed-resistance calculation omits this feedback. The burst example below uses that approximation explicitly, and the subsequent stall calculation restores it.

Datasheet thermal ratings are optimistic because they are measured on an unconstrained motor mounted to a large heat sink in free air at \(20^\circ\text{C}\) or \(25^\circ\text{C}\). Installed in a sealed chassis beside power electronics, battery packs, and other joints, the motor loses convective airflow, which raises \(\ThermalResistance\). Neighboring loads raise its local ambient above the test ambient before its own coils carry current. Thermal margins must therefore be budgeted from heating and cooling time constants measured with the enclosure sealed and adjacent loads at their \(\text{p99}\) power draw.

2.5.2 Transient thermal bursts and periodic duty cycles

Because thermal capacity integrates power over time, a peak torque14 or peak current rating is meaningless without four parameters: the initial winding temperature \(\WindingTemperature(0)\), the allowed duration \(\ActuatorOnTime\), the ambient \(\AmbientTemperature\), and the recovery time \(\ActuatorOffTime\). An actuator that can produce three times its continuous torque sustains it only while the integral of copper loss (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\)) stays within the margin below the insulation limit. From a cold winding, that can last tens of seconds, and from a heat-soaked one a fraction of that (notebook 2.5).

14 Duty Type and RMS Torque: IEC 60034-1 rates a motor for a duty type: S1 is continuous running at constant load long enough to reach thermal equilibrium, and S3 is an intermittent periodic duty of identical load-and-rest cycles too short to reach it (International Electrotechnical Commission 2022). Because copper loss grows with \(\MotorTorque^2\), the torque that heats the winding over a cycle short compared with the thermal time constant is the root-mean-square value \(\RmsMotorTorque = \sqrt{\frac{1}{\Time}\int_0^\Time \MotorTorque^2\,d\TimeInstant}\), and it is the RMS, not the peak, that must stay under the continuous rating. A peak rating means nothing without its duty type, which is why figure 6 draws the S1 and S3 regions separately.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2022. IEC 60034-1:2022: Rotating Electrical Machines, Part 1: Rating and Performance. International Electrotechnical Commission.

Napkin Math 2.5: Thermal accumulation across duty cycles
This example uses the arm’s sourced stand-in joint of notebook 2.4: a CSG-25-50 strain-wave gear (Harmonic Drive AG 2014) driven by a TQ-RoboDrive ILM 70x18 frameless motor (TQ-Systems 2014), whose rated current is \(\ContinuousCurrent =\) 6.7 A. The motor has effective aggregate loss resistance \(\EffectiveLossResistance =\) 0.495 Ω, lumped thermal capacitance \(\StatorThermalCapacitance =\) 120 J/K, and installed thermal resistance \(\ThermalResistance =\) 2.5 K/W (both thermal values illustrative, because the motor’s data sheet leaves them to the installation). It runs in an enclosed ambient \(\AmbientTemperature =\) 40 °C with Class F insulation rated to 155 °C. The thermal time constant is \(\ThermalTimeConstant = \ThermalResistance \StatorThermalCapacitance =\) 300 s.

Problem: How does the initial winding temperature change how long a threefold rated-current burst can last before the insulation limit?

Math:

  1. Continuous equilibrium: At \(\ContinuousCurrent =\) 6.7 A the winding settles at \(\RatedCurrentEquilibriumTemperature = \AmbientTemperature + \ContinuousCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\,\ThermalResistance =\) 95.6 °C, below the limit.
  2. Peak burst: At \(\PeakCurrent =\) 20.1 A the dissipation rises ninefold to \(\PeakCopperLossPower =\) 200.0 W, whose asymptote \(\EquilibriumWindingTemperature = \AmbientTemperature + \PeakCopperLossPower \ThermalResistance =\) 540.0 °C lies far above the limit.
  3. Survival horizons: With \(\WindingTemperature(\TimeInstant) = \EquilibriumWindingTemperature - (\EquilibriumWindingTemperature - \WindingTemperature(0))\,e^{-\TimeInstant/\ThermalTimeConstant}\), the time to reach the limit is \[\BurstSurvivalTime = \ThermalTimeConstant \ln\frac{\EquilibriumWindingTemperature - \WindingTemperature(0)}{\EquilibriumWindingTemperature - \WindingTemperatureLimit}\] which gives 78.4 s from a cold start at ambient and 43.1 s from a heat-soaked start at \(\RatedCurrentEquilibriumTemperature\).

Result: The heat-soaked joint holds the burst 45.1 percent less time than the cold one, so peak torque cannot be budgeted without tracking thermal history.

Systems insight: Peak torque is a stock of heat margin that thermal history spends, not a fixed rating. The admission check \(\EstimatedWindingTemperature \le \WindingTemperatureLimit\) must use the current estimate of the winding temperature, and these first-order horizons are optimistic, because the winding hotspot of the two-node model runs ahead of the lumped temperature.

TQ-Systems. 2014. ILM Series Frameless Servo Kits Data Sheet. TQ-RoboDrive product datasheet.
Harmonic Drive AG. 2014. Engineering Data CSG-2UH Units. Product documentation 1019642.

15 Periodic steady-state thermal peak: Over a repeating cycle of period \(\ActuatorCyclePeriod = \ActuatorOnTime + \ActuatorOffTime\), summing the heating and cooling exponentials gives the periodic peak temperature rise: \(\FiniteChange \PeriodicPeakWindingTemperature = \PeakCopperLossPower \ThermalResistance\frac{1 - e^{-\ActuatorOnTime/\ThermalTimeConstant}}{1 - e^{-\ActuatorCyclePeriod/\ThermalTimeConstant}}\). As the cycle period \(\ActuatorCyclePeriod \to 0\), this peak approaches the average-power screen (\(\ActuatorDutyCycle \PeakCopperLossPower \ThermalResistance\)); for any finite burst length, the peak winding temperature exceeds the average.

For repeated cycles of period \(\ActuatorCyclePeriod = \ActuatorOnTime + \ActuatorOffTime\), the engineering ceiling \(\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling =\) 140 °C sets a cycle-average power ceiling \(\CycleAveragePowerCeiling = (\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling - \AmbientTemperature)/\ThermalResistance =\) 40 W. With peak dissipation during \(\ActuatorOnTime\) and none during \(\ActuatorOffTime\), this average-power screen bounds the duty cycle \(\ActuatorDutyCycle = \ActuatorOnTime/\ActuatorCyclePeriod\) by \[\ActuatorDutyCycle \le \frac{\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling - \AmbientTemperature}{\PeakCopperLossPower \ThermalResistance} \tag{8}\] which here is 20.0 percent, so a 3 s burst calls for a recovery of \(\ActuatorOffTime = \ActuatorOnTime(1 - \ActuatorDutyCycle)/\ActuatorDutyCycle \approx\) 12 s. The average does not bound the hottest instant. Because heat arrives in seconds during bursts but dissipates over minutes during recovery, the peak transient hotspot temperature during periodic pulsing strictly exceeds the time-averaged calculation,15 reaching 142.0 °C (above the ceiling) before the rise of \(\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature)\) adds more. Average-power screening is optimistic, and a burst schedule is certified only against the transient hotspot trajectory.

2.5.3 Torque-speed operating envelopes and back-EMF roll-off

The burst calculation limits duration, while the back-EMF relation in section 2.4.2 limits torque at speed. A feasible maneuver must satisfy both. At low speed, the drive can supply the current needed for a burst, but the winding temperature limits how long it may do so. At higher speed, back-EMF consumes voltage headroom and can prevent that current from appearing at all. On the arm’s stand-in joint motor, voltage headroom and thermal history bound the usable torque-speed region simultaneously (figure 6). Because steady-state operation at full supply voltage splits the bus voltage between the resistive drop \(\TorqueProducingCurrent \PhaseResistance\) and the back-EMF \(\MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed\), rising torque demands more current, consumes a larger resistive voltage drop, and pulls the motor’s voltage-limited speed linearly down from 2,110 rpm at no load to 1,570 rpm at peak torque, while the burst that a cold winding sustains for 78.4 s reaches the insulation class in only 43.1 s from continuous-duty equilibrium.

The continuous-duty region (\(\Glyph{S}_1\)) permits sustained operation under the declared cooling conditions. The larger intermittent region (\(\Glyph{S}_3\)) permits periodic bursts only with the starting temperature, duration, and recovery interval established above. Both regions narrow when speed consumes the drive’s voltage headroom. Their intersection with the current thermal state, rather than the catalog peak, gives the available torque for the next maneuver.

A simulator that uses only a fixed torque clamp can therefore teach a policy an unavailable action: maximum torque while the rotor is already near its voltage-limited speed, or a repeated burst without recovery. The actuator model and permission limits must include speed and thermal history. Temperature-dependent winding resistance further shrinks the available region during a sustained hold.

The standard remedy in legged-robot sim-to-real transfer replaces the clamp with an actuator model identified on the hardware. For the series elastic actuators of the ANYmal quadruped, Hwangbo et al. (2019) trained a small network on a few minutes of logged joint data to map a short history of position errors and velocities to the torque the actuator actually delivered. The network then ran inside the simulator in place of an ideal actuator. The ideal model, which assumes any commanded torque appears instantly, failed to predict the measured torque. The same structure carries the envelope of this section into training: an actuator model that also takes speed, bus voltage, and winding temperature as inputs lets the simulator deny the torque the hardware cannot deliver.

↪ How actuator dynamics mismatch is measured and closed in the training simulator is developed in Physical Channels of the Sim-to-Real Gap.

2.5.4 Copper resistance feedback and stall thermal runaway

The copper-resistance rise calculated in section 2.5.1 must enter the runtime estimate as well as the design calculation. An external thermistor can lag the winding hotspot, so a thermal observer combines measured current with the characterized heating and cooling model. A discrete update of the lumped energy balance is: \[{\EstimatedWindingTemperature{}}_{k+1} = {\EstimatedWindingTemperature{}}_{k} + \frac{\FiniteChange \TimeInstant}{\StatorThermalCapacitance} \left[ {\TorqueProducingCurrent}_{k}^2 \ElectricalResistance({\EstimatedWindingTemperature{}}_{k}) - \frac{{\EstimatedWindingTemperature{}}_{k} - \AmbientTemperature}{\ThermalResistance} \right]\] where \(\ElectricalResistance({\EstimatedWindingTemperature{}}_{k}) = \ReferenceWindingResistance [1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient({\EstimatedWindingTemperature{}}_{k} - \CopperReferenceTemperature)]\). The update interval, model error, and temperature uncertainty must fit the thermal margin. As the estimate approaches its ceiling, the permission path reduces admissible effort or transfers the load to a qualified support mechanism. Removing holding current without securing a gravity-loaded arm would exchange a thermal fault for uncontrolled motion. A static hold shows how copper’s feedback changes the sustainable torque (notebook 2.6).

Napkin Math 2.6: Static holding torque and stall derating
The arm’s elbow, the same Panda-class stand-in joint as notebook 2.5, is asked to hold the arm’s maximum joint torque \(\HoldingTorque =\) 87 N·m at stall (\(\RotorAngularSpeed = 0\)) through its \(\GearReductionRatio =\) 50:1 strain-wave gear (\(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency =\) 0.85, illustrative). Its motor has \(\MotorTorqueConstant =\) 0.187 N·m/A and cold effective aggregate loss resistance \(\ReferenceWindingResistance =\) 0.495 Ω at \(\CopperReferenceTemperature =\) 20 °C. The illustrative installed thermal resistance is \(\ThermalResistance =\) 1.80 K/W, lower than the 2.5 K/W of the burst example and so a better-cooled installation. The Class F limit is 155 °C, and the limb ambient is \(\AmbientTemperature =\) 40 °C.

Problem: With copper’s resistance rising with temperature, where does the stalled winding settle, and what holding torque is thermally sustainable?

Math:

  1. Stall current: \(\MotorTorque = \HoldingTorque/(\GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio) =\) 2.05 N·m, so \(\TorqueProducingCurrent = \MotorTorque/\MotorTorqueConstant =\) 10.9 A and the cold dissipation is \(\ColdCopperLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ReferenceWindingResistance =\) 59.3 W.

  2. Runaway: With \(\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperature) = \ReferenceWindingResistance[1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient(\WindingTemperature - \CopperReferenceTemperature)]\), the steady rise satisfies \[\JouleanTemperatureRise = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ReferenceWindingResistance \left[1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient(\AmbientTemperature - \CopperReferenceTemperature + \JouleanTemperatureRise)\right] \ThermalResistance\] Rearranging terms yields \(\JouleanTemperatureRise = \frac{\TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ReferenceWindingResistance [1 + \CopperTemperatureCoefficient(\AmbientTemperature - \CopperReferenceTemperature)] \ThermalResistance}{1 - \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ReferenceWindingResistance \ThermalResistance \CopperTemperatureCoefficient}\). The denominator establishes the thermal runaway criterion: if the positive feedback loop gain satisfies \(\TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \ReferenceWindingResistance \ThermalResistance \CopperTemperatureCoefficient \ge 1\), this fixed-current, linear-resistance model cannot settle to a stable thermal equilibrium. Above one, heat generation increases with temperature faster than heat removal, producing exponential growth. At exactly one, the temperature-dependent terms cancel and the remaining positive heating causes linear growth. In this stall scenario, the feedback factor is 0.4196 \(< 1\), so temperature settles at a finite (yet fatal) equilibrium given by \(\JouleanTemperatureRise =\) 115.16 \(+\) 0.4196 \(\JouleanTemperatureRise\). This yields \(\JouleanTemperatureRise =\) 198.4 K and drives the winding to 238.4 °C, far above its rating.

  3. Derating: At the limit the allowable rise is 115 K, so the sustainable dissipation is \(\SustainableCopperLossPower = \FiniteChange \WindingTemperatureLimit/\ThermalResistance =\) 63.89 W. At the hot resistance \(\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperatureLimit) \approx\) 0.758 Ω, that allows \(\ContinuousCurrent = \sqrt{\SustainableCopperLossPower/\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperatureLimit)} \approx\) 9.18 A and a hold of \(\SustainableHoldingTorque = \GearboxTransmissionEfficiency \GearReductionRatio \MotorTorqueConstant \ContinuousCurrent \approx\) 73.0 N·m.

Result: Held without control, the stall drives the winding toward 238.4 °C, and staying within the insulation rating requires a 16.1 percent derating, from 87 N·m to 73.0 N·m.

Systems insight: At stall the motor does no mechanical work, so every watt it draws becomes heat, and copper’s rising resistance makes that heat grow with temperature. Continuous gravity holding above the thermally sustainable torque therefore needs another support mechanism, such as a friction brake or counterweight, in the premise the permission path admits; holding within that torque can remain electrically sustained under the stated thermal conditions.

Thermal capacity governs how long an actuator can sustain torque before winding insulation degrades or thermal runaway occurs, bounding continuous holds and transient bursts by the plant’s ability to reject heat. Motor windings, however, do not generate those thermal loads in isolation. Delivering the current pulses that drive these torque trajectories simultaneously stresses the upstream power distribution network, shifting the engineering constraint from heat dissipation in copper to transient voltage stability on the shared direct-current bus.

2.6 Electrical Power Integrity

When the mobile manipulator swerves hard to avoid a collision, every drive motor, joint inverter, and accelerator demands peak current at once from one direct-current distribution bus. In simulation, current flows from an idealized source. On hardware, delivery is bounded by finite capacity, source impedance, and transient voltage stability. A machine that browns out mid-motion fails physically; the electrical supply is a shared resource.

Rail sag, regenerative overvoltage, and thermal accumulation are manifestations of one bounded energy network on different time scales. Of the three, voltage moves fastest, within microseconds to milliseconds whenever current demand exceeds what the source can deliver at that instant. Compute, sensing, and motor inverters draw from a common bus, so when a policy commands coordinated accelerations across several joints, the sum of their currents changes the voltage delivered to every node on the machine.

2.6.1 Power distribution impedance, transient droop, and brownout resets

The mobile machine’s battery, harnesses, connectors, and protection switches all contribute source impedance.16 Local capacitors supply part of a sudden load increase, so current drawn by the loads need not equal current arriving through the harness. For a source feeding one bus node through resistance and inductance, \[\SourceVoltage(\TimeInstant)-\DcBusVoltage(\TimeInstant)=\PowerRailResistance{}\SourceCurrent(\TimeInstant)+\PowerRailInductance\frac{d\SourceCurrent}{d\TimeInstant} \tag{9}\] where \(\PowerRailResistance\) is the lumped source resistance, \(\PowerRailInductance\) is the loop inductance, and \(\SourceCurrent\) is the current through that source path. The local capacitor bank \(\DcBusBulkCapacitance\) supplies the difference between load current \(\LoadCurrent\) and source current. In the ideal-capacitor model, charge removed from that bank lowers the same bus voltage: \[\DcBusVoltage(0)-\DcBusVoltage(\TimeInstant)=\frac{1}{\DcBusBulkCapacitance}\int_0^\TimeInstant\left[\LoadCurrent(\IntegrationTimeInstant)-\SourceCurrent(\IntegrationTimeInstant)\right]d\IntegrationTimeInstant.\] These equations jointly determine the voltage response; the capacitor’s voltage change is not an additional drop to add to equation 9.

16 Source Resistance: The lumped source resistance is \(\PowerRailResistance = \BatteryCellResistance + \HarnessConnectorResistance + \ProtectionSwitchResistance\), the sum of battery cell, harness and connector, and protection-switch on-state resistances. The cell term is the one that moves. It rises as the pack ages and as it cools, so a sustained screen passed at commissioning can fail on a cold morning years later. That drift belongs in the rail’s limit record as a condition, with a detector that measures the drop under load.

↪ Sizing the bus capacitance against that droop, and the undervoltage lockout it must avoid, is developed in DC Bus Power Distribution and Energy Absorption.

Compute and motion must be budgeted together because they share this path. An obstacle response can require fresh inference at the same time as a large drive-current change, so testing each load separately misses the combined step that the supply must support. Harness inductance limits how quickly source current can rise. Local capacitors supply the initial current deficit, while harness resistance continues to lower the rail after source current catches up with a sustained load. Once the currents settle, the inductive voltage is zero and the sustained drop is \(\SourceCurrent\PowerRailResistance\). The worked example below screens that sustained condition. It does not establish the minimum voltage during the transient. If the actual voltage crosses a regulator’s undervoltage threshold, computation or communication may stop or reset. Power qualification must establish the voltage at the supplied devices during coincident load and the physical response if those devices lose power.

2.6.2 Unpowered states and unmanaged fallback dynamics

A brownout reset turns the electrical fault into a mechanical one. Voltage supervisors pull the reset line on microcontrollers and gate drivers, clocks halt, register configurations vanish, and communication buses float. An acceleration command issued just before the reset stays physically active until the gate-driver stage collapses. If the driver then floats, phase current ceases, torque falls to zero, and the body coasts under gravity and inertia into workpieces or hardstops. If it instead shorts the low-side switches for dynamic braking, back-EMF (\(\BackEmfVoltage = \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed\)) produces an immediate, abrupt counter-torque that jerks the mechanics at the joint limits.

Power removal is an unmanaged state transition, not an automatic return to safety. Cutting power leaves an actuator in one of three unpowered states, namely free coasting, phase-short dynamic braking, or spring-engaged mechanical braking, and each enforces a distinct mechanical behavior. Free coasting simply allows gravity to accelerate unconstrained joints without any resistance. An electromechanical friction brake requires 30 ms to 80 ms for its mechanical spring to overcome magnetic coil decay and engage its friction pads, during which gravity accelerates an overhead arm downward.

Phase-short dynamic braking generates a counter-torque that at low speed grows in proportion to angular velocity (\(\MotorTorque \approx \frac{\MotorTorqueConstant \MotorBackEmfConstant}{\PhaseResistance} \RotorAngularSpeed\)) and gives zero holding torque at zero velocity. The proportionality does not last. As speed rises, the winding inductance limits the short-circuit current. The shorted winding is a resistance and an inductance driven by the back-EMF \(\MotorBackEmfConstant\RotorAngularSpeed\), which alternates at the electrical speed \(\RotorPolePairCount\RotorAngularSpeed\). An inductance opposes an alternating current in proportion to its frequency, with reactance \(\RotorPolePairCount\RotorAngularSpeed \PhaseInductance\), and that opposition adds to the resistance in quadrature. The current is therefore \(\MotorBackEmfConstant\RotorAngularSpeed/\sqrt{\PhaseResistance^2 + (\RotorPolePairCount\RotorAngularSpeed \PhaseInductance)^2}\). The braking torque, the power dissipated in \(\PhaseResistance\) divided by \(\RotorAngularSpeed\), is \(\MotorTorqueConstant \MotorBackEmfConstant \RotorAngularSpeed \PhaseResistance/(\PhaseResistance^2 + (\RotorPolePairCount\RotorAngularSpeed \PhaseInductance)^2)\). That torque peaks where the electrical speed \(\RotorPolePairCount \RotorAngularSpeed\) equals \(\PhaseResistance/\PhaseInductance\) and falls beyond it, so above that speed a phase short brakes less the faster the joint turns. For the base’s hub motors that peak lies at 177.3 rpm of the wheel, above the 107.7 rpm of the drive limit, so braking torque still rises across the base’s speed range, though not strictly in proportion to speed. A fast, high-ratio joint motor has no such guarantee.

Phase-short braking peaks at 177.3 rpm, above the base’s 107.7 rpm limit.

Industrial machine stop categories and drive safety functions sequence these states. The machinery standard defines three stop categories (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016a): remove power at once, stop under control and then remove power, or stop under control and keep power applied. Each category still requires specific physical evidence before a safety case can credit it (Stop functions and physical response). Beneath every category lies one physical fact. Once torque is removed, a joint enters one of the three unpowered states above. A drive’s Safe Torque Off (STO) therefore leaves a moving joint in free coast until a brake engages. The trajectory a machine follows after an electrical cutoff must be measured under full mechanical payload, not deduced from circuit diagrams. The choice of which safe fallback state the machine should enter belongs to system safety architecture, but the physical dynamics of an unpowered joint are unalterable mechanical constraints of the body.

↪ The placement of Safe Torque Off in the machine’s hardwired interlock is developed in Actuation Authority.

2.6.3 Regenerative overvoltage surges and dynamic brake choppers

Regenerative deceleration drives the bus toward its opposite boundary, overvoltage, as part of the machine’s kinetic energy returns electrically. Returned braking energy needs an absorption or dissipation path, and a battery cannot be assumed to accept it under every state of charge, temperature, or protection state. If the battery management system disconnects charge input, the remaining bus components must handle the regenerated energy without exceeding their voltage ratings.

Under the conservative assumption that the battery management system has disconnected and the local bus capacitance \(\DcBusBulkCapacitance\) must absorb the entire returned kinetic energy \(\RegeneratedBrakingEnergy\) without dissipation, the terminal DC bus voltage rises to: \[\FinalBusVoltage = \sqrt{\InitialBusVoltage^2 + \frac{2 \RegeneratedBrakingEnergy}{\DcBusBulkCapacitance}} \tag{10}\] where \(\InitialBusVoltage\) is the bus voltage when deceleration begins (DC Bus Power Distribution and Energy Absorption derives this energy balance). The worst case is a full pack, the state in which the battery management system refuses further charge.

The capacitor-only endpoint is a screening calculation: if it exceeds a component rating, the stop requires another energy path. A dynamic brake chopper switches an absorption resistor across the bus when voltage reaches a chosen clamp threshold. Its resistor must handle both the peak regenerated power and the pulse energy, with enough recovery between stops. The worked bus calculation below sizes the peak-power requirement for the warehouse machine.

Power integrity is an architectural constraint. The design sets coordinated power budgets and current slew-rate limits across accelerators and drives. The permission path arbitrates simultaneous multi-axis accelerations so that their \(d\ConductorCurrent/d\TimeInstant\) transients do not compound. Brake choppers protect the inverters from regenerative feedback during emergency stops. Notebook 2.7 works both halves of that budget, the bus’s sag and the stop’s regenerative surge, on the mobile manipulator’s 48 V drive bus.

Napkin Math 2.7: DC bus voltage sag and regenerative surge
The mobile manipulator’s lithium-ion pack feeds a 47.7 V nominal drive bus, with the voltage envelope of a commercial 48 V-class mobile robot’s pack (Mobile Industrial Robots 2025). The bus carries the wheel drives, the arm’s joint drives, and a 48-to-24 V converter that supplies the application processor and control electronics. The converter shuts off when its input falls to 33 V (MEAN WELL 2026).

Problem: Under the coincident current step of an inference burst and the drive motors, and then a stop from the drive limit, does the bus remain within the operating limits of the converter and the motor inverters?

Math:

  1. Parameters:
    • Machine mass, travel speed, and credible deceleration: \(\Mass =\) 368 kg (upper bound, full tote rack), \(\SpeedAtDetection =\) 1.5 m/s, \(\CredibleBrakingDeceleration =\) 2 m/s²
    • Bus characteristics: pack voltage from 54 V at full charge to 42 V depleted, source resistance \(\PowerRailResistance =\) 60 mΩ (illustrative), bulk link capacitance \(\DcBusBulkCapacitance =\) 22,000 μF
    • Operating limits: converter lockout \(\ConverterLockoutVoltage =\) 33 V, converter continuous input maximum 67.2 V, inverter switch rating \(\SwitchBreakdownVoltage =\) 100 V
  2. Sustained-load screen:
    • As an illustrative coincident load, the two drive motors and the arm’s joint drives add 68.6 A while the application processor, drawing its 60 W thermal design power (TDP) through the converter at 91 percent efficiency, adds 1.4 A. Their aggregate increase is \(\FiniteChange \ConductorCurrent =\) 70 A. Assume the stated pack voltage is the unloaded source voltage and this coincident demand is the total load in the screen.
    • After source current settles to the increased load, the additional resistive drop is: \[\Delta V_{\text{sustained}} = \Delta I R_{\text{bus}} = 4.2 V\]
    • The resulting sustained bus voltage at full charge and when depleted is: \[V_{\text{bus, sustained}} = 54 V - 4.2 V = 49.8 V \text{ (full)}, \quad 42 V - 4.2 V = 37.8 V \text{ (depleted)}\]
    • Even from the depleted pack, the bus stays 4.8 V above the converter’s lockout; it would take a 150 A step to spend the 9 V between the depleted pack and the lockout. This screen does not establish the transient minimum, which inductance and capacitor depletion push lower.
  3. Regenerative surge:
    • During a stop from the drive limit, the loaded machine’s kinetic energy must be dissipated: \[E_k = \tfrac{1}{2} m v^2 = \tfrac{1}{2}\,(368 kg)(1.5 m/s)^2 = 414 J\]
    • A full pack is the worst case, because that is when the battery management system refuses charge. If it disconnects charge input and an illustrative 75 percent of kinetic energy returns to the bus (\(\RegeneratedBrakingEnergy =\) 310.5 J), an unprotected, lossless capacitor-only energy balance would end at: \[V_{\text{final}} = \sqrt{V_{\text{full}}^2 + \frac{2 E_{\text{regen}}}{C_{\text{bus}}}} \approx 176.5 V\]
    • Failure mechanism: Raising the capacitor from full charge to the 60 V clamp absorbs only 7.5 J of the 310.5 J, so long before the bus reached that endpoint it would cross the converter’s input maximum and then the 100 V switch rating.
  4. Dynamic braking clamp:
    • Under constant deceleration over \(\StoppingDuration =\) 750 ms, speed falls linearly, so the regenerated power, force times speed, falls linearly from its peak to zero. The peak is twice the average \(\RegeneratedBrakingEnergy/\StoppingDuration\): \[P_{\text{regen, peak}} \approx \frac{2 E_{\text{regen}}}{t_{\text{stop}}} = 828 W\]
    • The clamp \(\ChopperClampVoltage =\) 60 V sits above the full-charge voltage, so it never fires while charging, and within the converter’s input range. To hold it there, the brake chopper must divert this peak power through an absorption resistor. A resistor across the bus at the clamp voltage dissipates \(\ChopperClampVoltage^2/\BrakeChopperResistance\), which must be at least the peak power: \[R_{\text{brake}} \le \frac{V_{\text{clamp}}^2}{P_{\text{regen, peak}}} \approx 4.35 Ω\]

Result: The sustained-load screen leaves the bus at 49.8 V from a full pack and 37.8 V from a depleted one, 4.8 V above the converter lockout. Unbuffered regenerative braking would drive the bus past the converter’s input maximum and the 100 V switch rating, which calls for a dynamic brake chopper whose resistor is no larger than 4.35 Ω and absorbs an 828 W peak (satisfied by an illustrative 3.9 Ω, 1 kW component).

Systems insight: Mechanical motion and electrical power are bidirectionally coupled. Acceleration requires a supply that sustains the coincident current demand; deceleration requires an adequate absorption or dissipation path. On this 48 V bus the depleted pack still sits 9 V above the converter lockout, more than twice this step’s resistive drop, so the sustained screen passes. The transient minimum, pulse energy, repetition, cooling, and component tolerances still require qualification.

MEAN WELL. 2026. DDR-240 Series 240 W DIN Rail Type DC-DC Converter Specification. Product datasheet.
Mobile Industrial Robots. 2025. MiR250 Specifications. Product specification.

The bus must stay between the 33 V lockout and the clamp; an unclamped stop reaches 176.5 V.

Checkpoint 2.2: Freshness, stopping, heat, and supply

The actuator budget was checked in checkpoint 2.1. Before measuring these limits on an assembled machine, verify your understanding of the other four:

Preserving power integrity ensures that transient acceleration spikes do not trigger brownout resets and that regenerative braking does not overvoltage the bus, completing the analytical formulation of the five physical budgets. Calculating these envelopes from datasheets and circuit models, however, cannot guarantee operational safety on a physical robot. In deployment, source impedances rise as batteries age, contact friction varies across facility floors, and enclosed packaging degrades thermal dissipation. Defending these budgets in production requires measuring what the integrated hardware actually delivers under live operational stress.

2.7 Measuring a Machine’s Own Limits

A brake that stops an unloaded base on a cool laboratory floor has not established how the loaded mobile manipulator stops after repeated maneuvers. Characterization must connect each calculation to measurements under the conditions in which the machine will use it. A dynamometer, a test stand that applies a controlled mechanical load, isolates actuator behavior, just as the instrumented propulsion test stand in figure 7 evaluates an assembled electromechanical powertrain under live aerodynamic and structural loads. Integrated trials then establish what changes when the actuator shares a chassis, cooling path, and power supply with the rest of the machine. A defensible limit states what was measured, how uncertain the result is, and which conditions must remain true for a later decision to rely on it.

2.7.1 Dynamometer testing and instrument intrusion

On a dynamometer, a controlled load machine resists the actuator while torque, speed, current, and voltage are measured on a shared timebase. Four tests connect the earlier models to measurable parameters:

  • Torque and back-EMF transduction (\(\MotorTorqueConstant\) and \(\MotorBackEmfConstant\)): By commanding the inverter’s Field-Oriented Control (FOC) current loop across a sweep of quadrature phase currents \(\TorqueProducingCurrent\) while the dynamometer absorbs torque at constant speed, engineers measure electromechanical torque transduction: \[\MotorTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \TorqueProducingCurrent\] Driving the unpowered motor at known speed and measuring open-circuit voltage supplies the corresponding back-EMF constant \(\MotorBackEmfConstant = \BackEmfVoltage / \RotorAngularSpeed\). These tests establish the conversion factors used in section 2.4.2.

  • Electrical rise time and inductive bandwidth (\(\ElectricalTimeConstant = \PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance\)): With the rotor held so that the test current produces no torque, the inverter applies a small voltage step \(\StepTestVoltage\) across the winding. The current response: \[\InstantaneousPhaseCurrent(\TimeInstant) = \frac{\StepTestVoltage}{\PhaseResistance}\left(1 - e^{-\TimeInstant/\ElectricalTimeConstant}\right)\] reveals the electrical time constant. The step must stay small, because its steady current \(\StepTestVoltage/\PhaseResistance\) flows through a winding of low resistance: the full 42 V of a depleted pack across a hub motor’s 0.7 Ω would drive 60 A, 2.4× the motor’s peak current. The current probe and acquisition path must resolve the rise; otherwise the instrument smooths the response being measured and hides the actuator limit.

  • Enclosed thermal resistance (\(\ThermalResistance\)) and continuous torque ceilings: A heating trial under the installed cooling conditions measures the winding temperature rise and loss power, establishing \(\ThermalResistance = \JouleanTemperatureRise / \WindingLossPower\). The continuous torque ceiling from section 2.5.1 is then: \[\ContinuousTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \sqrt{\frac{\WindingTemperatureLimit - \AmbientTemperature}{\ElectricalResistance(\WindingTemperatureLimit) \ThermalResistance}}\] The test must reproduce the enclosure and neighboring heat loads under which that ceiling will be used. An open-bench rating overstates continuous capacity whenever the installed shroud restricts airflow or adjacent actuators warm the mounting frame.

  • Backdrive friction and impact dynamics: By driving the actuator output shaft with the dynamometer load motor, the testbed measures the torque needed to backdrive the joint across speed, load, and temperature: \[\ExternalJointTorque = \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia \JointAcceleration + \JointFrictionTorque\] The measured response establishes whether the transmission can yield under the intended external load and maps the friction term that section 2.4.5 shows is not a constant. Contact qualification must additionally exercise the relevant compliance and impact conditions. The backdrive model alone does not predict tooth failure.

Measurement can change the plant. A series current shunt lowers the supplied voltage, and an embedded temperature probe changes a local heat-flow path. These effects belong in the uncertainty budget along with sensor resolution, bandwidth, and clock alignment.

The sensor mechanisms in section 2.2.1 also affect characterization. Velocity estimated from encoder samples by backward differencing trades resolution against latency, and that trade-off must enter the reported uncertainty and response time. Vibration above the sensor’s sampled bandwidth can likewise appear as slower motion: \[\AliasedVibrationFrequency = |\VibrationFrequency - \AliasingHarmonicIndex \SamplingRate|\] The integer \(\AliasingHarmonicIndex\) identifies the sampling-frequency multiple that folds the vibration into the observed band. Qualification must distinguish that measurement artifact from the motion used to establish the limit.

A limit means nothing without the context of its test. Every recorded limit states the payload, ambient temperature, supply voltage, enclosure state, firmware version, and concurrent computational workload of the run. A thermal time constant measured on an open bench does not transfer to the same actuator in a sealed fairing in sunlight, because enclosing the motor raises \(\ThermalResistance\) and with it \(\ThermalTimeConstant = \ThermalResistance \StatorThermalCapacitance\). The hotter ambient also shrinks the margin below the insulation rating. A rail characterization that omits whether vision-language model inference (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023) was running during motor stalls describes an idle board rather than the combined draw of full compute and peak torque.

Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Joseph Dabis, Chelsea Finn, Keerthana Gopalakrishnan, et al. 2023. “RT-1: Robotics Transformer for Real-World Control at Scale.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.025.
Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Xi Chen, Krzysztof Choromanski, Tianli Ding, et al. 2023. “RT-2: Vision-Language-Action Models Transfer Web Knowledge to Robotic Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.15818.

2.7.2 Zero-failure bounds and wear over life

The absence of a failure during testing bounds the failure rate statistically; it does not establish safety. If the mobile manipulator completes \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount =\) 3,000 loaded stops without a brownout or an overrun, the data support only a per-stop failure rate below roughly \(3/\ZeroFailureTrialCount\), the rule of three, which is about one stop in 1,000 at 95 percent confidence. The bound holds only if the trials are independent and stationary, so that one trial does not influence another and the failure probability stays the same from trial to trial.

↪ The rule of three, and the trial campaigns it sizes, is developed in Confidence from a Finite Campaign.

Thermal history and wear can change the conditions between trials:

  • Heating across consecutive trials: Repeated stops heat the windings, changing resistance and the available torque response. Trials taken at different starting temperatures do not characterize the same operating condition.
  • Rising battery resistance over life: Battery aging increases source resistance, reducing the voltage margin under a current step.
  • Growing gearbox backlash: Transmission wear changes reversal response and tracking error, invalidating a limit measured on a less worn assembly.

These changes make characterization a standing obligation. Lifecycle degradation changes the conditions behind a limit, so its record must identify when recalibration or a reduced operating envelope is required.

2.7.3 The six elements of a limit record

The stopping and power calculations become usable by other layers only when their assumptions travel with their values. A limit record carries those assumptions together with the evidence that supports the limit. The learned proposer uses the limits to constrain candidate actions, while the independent permission path checks them before actuation. Recording a value does not certify it: the illustrative entries below still require measurements and uncertainty estimates before they can support operation. The six elements in table 3 make that missing evidence visible for the mobile manipulator’s credible deceleration, the drive torque ceiling that must support it, and the drive bus’s undervoltage lockout.

↪ The handoff record that carries each limit record to the permission path is developed in The Handoffs Matrix.

Two of the elements are easily conflated. Uncertainty describes what is not known about the measurement (observation noise, instrument tolerance, sensor bandwidth, and calibration drift). Margin is the physical clearance the machine defends against what is not known. The provenance element then says how far the value itself can be trusted, by naming its evidence category. Every number that governs a machine falls in one of five categories: a direct measurement, a value derived from measurements through a stated model, a datasheet rating, an illustrative example, or a budget the designer chose. Only the first two can support a safety margin or a timeout. A value computed from datasheet inputs, such as the drive torque ceiling below, keeps the datasheet’s weaker standing until measurements replace its inputs. A chosen budget, such as the protective clearance \(\ProtectiveClearanceMargin\), binds only once it is checked against the measured number it must fit.

The detector element matters most once the machine leaves the test stand, because operation can carry a limit outside the conditions under which it was established. The saturation duration against which the Schiaparelli lander’s inertial unit had been accepted was such a limit. The lander’s loss in 2016 shows what follows when a limit’s conditions are exceeded in operation and nothing downstream checks the result.

War Story 2.1: ExoMars Schiaparelli IMU saturation (2016)
Context: On October 19, 2016, the European Space Agency’s ExoMars Schiaparelli lander executed its atmospheric entry and descent toward the Martian surface, relying on an onboard Guidance, Navigation, and Control (GNC) computer to fuse Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU) gyroscopes with a Doppler radar altimeter (ESA Investigation Board 2017).

Mechanism: About three minutes after atmospheric entry, the parachute inflated and set the lander oscillating. The IMU measured a pitch rate larger than expected and raised a saturation flag, which persisted longer than the 15 ms assumed when the unit was accepted. While the flag was set, the GNC software integrated the saturation threshold rate as though it were the true rate, although the lander was in reality oscillating, and its attitude estimate drifted by about \(165^{\circ}\), nearly upside down. When the radar altimeter later returned valid slant ranges, the software projected them onto that attitude and computed a negative altitude. No onboard check tested that altitude for plausibility.

Impact: Taking the negative altitude as a valid estimate, the guidance logic released the parachute and backshell, fired the braking thrusters for \(3\text{ seconds}\) instead of the planned \(30\text{ seconds}\), and activated its on-ground systems as if it had landed. The lander was still about \(3.7\text{ km}\) above the surface. It fell freely for about half a minute and struck the ground at about \(150\text{ m/s}\) (\(540\text{ km/h}\)).

Response: The ESA inquiry board recommended robust sanity checks in the onboard software, among them that altitude cannot be negative and cannot change from \(3.7\text{ km}\) to a negative value in one second, and a design that handles IMU saturation explicitly, for example by inhibiting IMU inputs during parachute deployment.

Systems lesson: A physical body cannot transcend the saturation ranges of its sensory transducers. When a sensor’s physical limit (a gyroscope’s rate ceiling or an accelerometer’s dynamic range) is exceeded, a state estimator that integrates the saturated measurement as if it were valid drifts away from the physical state. The saturation duration against which the IMU had been accepted was a limit with conditions, and once descent exceeded them, no detector checked the estimate before it commanded an irreversible action.

ESA Investigation Board. 2017. ExoMars 2016 - Schiaparelli Anomaly Inquiry. European Space Agency.
Checkpoint 2.3: Limit records

A limit governs a decision only together with the conditions, evidence, and detector that travel with it.

Measured and recorded one at a time, the five limits describe the body budget by budget, each under its own conditions. In operation, however, a single maneuver draws on all five budgets at once, and a change in one budget’s state can erode the margin of another. Which limit binds first therefore depends on both the machine’s physical class and the specific command under evaluation.

2.8 Which Physical Budget Binds First

A single command from an action-chunking policy to accelerate a loaded mobile manipulator draws concurrently against all five physical budgets, which is why the simultaneous admission invariant of section 2.1 admits it only when every margin is non-negative at once. The command executes against a camera frame that has already aged during neural inference, so that age enters the pre-brake delay. At the same time, the acceleration injects kinetic energy into the chassis, which friction brakes or regenerative drives must eventually dissipate. Generating that motion forces the arm’s geared joints to accelerate reflected rotor inertia, while the base’s direct-drive hubs add their rotating inertia to the stopping load. The phase currents that produce those torques dump resistive Joule heat into stator windings, which shed thermal energy only over minutes. The same currents pull transient current from the shared DC rail that powers the onboard compute host. Each of the five budgets pairs a governing physical law against the software assumption it invalidates, exposing a characteristic vulnerability by machine class and demanding a hardware-enforced runtime invariant (table 4).

2.8.1 Which budget binds in each machine class
