A mobile manipulator integrates two of the machine classes from Three Machine Classes onto a single chassis: a Class 1 wheeled base governed primarily by chassis momentum and stopping distance, and a Class 2 articulated arm constrained by reflected rotor inertia, contact compliance, and static holding heat. While the classes identify the primary vulnerabilities, the instantaneous maneuver determines which budget binds first. For instance, holding a heavy payload in a cantilevered pose can drive joint windings toward their thermal dissipation limit over tens of seconds. Conversely, a sudden multi-axis acceleration burst coupled with full neural network execution can deplete electrical bus margins within milliseconds. The coupled calculations below compare the evidence behind the running machine’s limits and identify a stopping qualification priority. They do not establish an ordering of all five physical margins for an unspecified maneuver.

2.8.2 Cross-budget coupling and permission rail isolation

When an autonomous agent commands an aggressive multi-axis acceleration, the physical margins do not degrade in isolation. They deplete together. Drive inverters draw substantial current to overcome both chassis inertia and reflected rotor inertia. This transient current surge causes the shared DC bus voltage to sag, even as resistive losses (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\)) steadily deposit thermal energy into motor windings. Because motor phase currents are pulse-width modulated by inverters and do not map directly to battery draw, systems architects must evaluate input current at the primary DC bus. This coincident load step establishes the true operating baseline across all subsystems.

On the 48 V drive bus, the sustained electrical screen passes under its stated assumptions. As shown in the coincident power step of notebook 2.7, a depleted battery pack at 42 V sags to 37.8 V under the coincident load step. Even under that sustained step, the rail keeps a 4.8 V buffer above the 33 V undervoltage lockout (UVLO) threshold of the 48-to-24 V converter, although the sustained screen does not establish the transient minimum.

The stopping dynamics expose a concrete qualification priority. Under emergency braking, the drive motors clear the required deceleration by only 16.6 percent, and only with phase current at the motor’s catalog peak. The chassis tip-over boundary has not been measured at all. For the arm, the optimistic stand-in thermal model gives a heat-soaked joint a threefold current burst of 43.1 s before its winding reaches the insulation rating. Additionally, the chassis stopping distance at the full 1.5 m/s travel limit leaves 357.5 mm of clearance to accommodate perception latency, communication jitter, and tracking variance. The whole stopping budget inherits the credible-deceleration premise, which rests on the peak current limit and the unmeasured center of gravity, so that premise requires qualification before relying on the stop. The transient rail minimum and installed arm thermal response remain unresolved too. Ranking the budgets requires a specified maneuver and duration, with comparably qualified limits for each.

Modern SoC architectures deploy dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) algorithms to regulate die temperature and energy consumption. While DVFS protects silicon from thermal degradation, its timing penalties propagate directly into physical workspace clearance: \[\text{SoC thermal dissipation} \longrightarrow \text{DVFS clock throttling} \longrightarrow \text{vision latency stretch} \longrightarrow \text{observation age inflation} \longrightarrow \text{pre-brake travel expansion} \longrightarrow \text{clearance margin erosion}\] When sustained motor currents and high-throughput perception pipelines elevate temperatures across the shared enclosure, the host kernel throttles the clock frequencies of onboard CPU and GPU accelerators. On the running machine, this thermal intervention stretches the navigation backbone that the stopping budget charges, not the grasp path of table 1, from 22 ms to 33 ms. This delay adds 11 ms directly to the end-to-end observation age \(\ObservationAge\), inflating the pre-braking reaction time \(\PrebrakeDelay\). When traveling at the maximum base velocity of 1.5 m/s, this algorithmic latency injects 16.5 mm of unmonitored chassis travel before emergency brakes can even begin mechanical engagement. In this manner, a standard operating-system power policy designed for digital silicon silently shrinks the robot’s stopping margin.

↪ Thermal clock throttling on the shared die, and the placement decisions that bound its effect on the safety path, are developed in Thermal and Timing Coupling.

Surviving a sustained bus voltage drop does not qualify the shared power rail to support safety-critical control channels. Transient dips during motor reversals remain difficult to bound completely, and electrical faults such as an open circuit breaker, a battery management system (BMS) overcurrent disconnect, or a blown DC-DC stage drop bus voltage to zero instantly. The running machine addresses these common supply faults with a dual-rail architecture that decouples motor dynamics from safety enforcement. In this design, the primary shared bus powers high-current traction inverters and steps down voltage for the primary application processor. An isolated, reverse-blocked permission rail separately powers the safety microcontroller, deterministic fieldbus transceivers, and holding-brake release coils. Under the drive-rail faults covered by its isolation and hold-up qualification, this separate supply preserves the permission loads. It does not make the detector, controller, or communication path infallible.

The permission rail incorporates local capacitive energy storage to preserve control availability during a loss of its upstream supply. For the stated constant 70 W permission load, ideal energy sizing gives an onboard 2.025 F supercapacitor bank 140 J of usable energy between its minimum nominal charge voltage (21.5 V) and regulator dropout (18 V), targeting 2 s of hold-up. Establishing a minimum available duration requires qualification of initial charge, capacitance tolerance and aging, conversion losses, equivalent series resistance, current demand, and regulator behavior. The 750 ms stop from the drive limit plus the 80 ms slow end of spring-brake engagement is a comparison for the powered stopping model only. Total main-battery failure does not establish that this model remains available. The qualified control interval must cover detection and decision, the duration of the response available under the fault, and brake engagement when those stages are sequential, or their actual sequence when they overlap. The Fallback Ladder checks the hold-up against the longest stop its fallback ladder commands.

Under the qualified rail and intact detector and controller assumptions, maintaining permission-rail power preserves the opportunity to detect power loss and initiate a response, but it does not supply the mechanical energy or high-current paths necessary for controlled regenerative braking. A controlled, powered stop requires intact inverter switching, sufficient bus voltage, and an operational energy dump, such as a dynamic brake chopper or a regenerative battery sink, to absorb kinetic deceleration energy. When a catastrophic power fault removes the supply or absorption path needed for a powered stop, that stopping model no longer applies. Under such conditions, stopping distances must be governed by the unpowered physical fallbacks analyzed in section 2.6.2. A held-up permission rail does not change what the mechanical brakes deliver. Powering their release coils from that rail lets the permission logic choose when the spring brakes engage, but it does not give them the deceleration of a powered drive stop. Systems architects must treat stopping performance as conditional: isolate the safety supervisory rail, and then characterize the exact deceleration capabilities that remain active across every distinct electrical fault state. Downstream runtime enforcement and hardware qualification must preserve these underlying electrical premises.

2.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Physical limits rarely respect subsystem boundaries. In embodied machine learning systems, an architecture whose individual component budgets balance cleanly on paper can fail catastrophically when thermal rise, bus latency, power supply sag, and mechanical dynamics interact under compound operational load.

Fallacy: Model inference time defines the measurement freshness latency.

Consider an engineering team that compresses its vision backbone, cutting neural network inference time from \(30\text{ ms}\) to \(10\text{ ms}\). While this optimization provides a threefold compute speedup, the physical robot still executes actions based on visual state that traversed camera exposure, sensor readout, DMA transfers, and kernel queuing delays. Once the model outputs an action, that command must traverse a real-time network fieldbus before motor drive inverters can slew coil currents and produce physical force. A \(45\text{ ms}\) scheduling or transport jitter spike elsewhere along this pipeline eclipses the \(20\text{ ms}\) compute gain, consuming safety margins before the robot can react. Real-time control requires budgeting and bounding the worst-case tail of the entire capture-to-onset pipeline across synchronized hardware clocks, rather than certifying loop safety from isolated model benchmarks.

Architect’s countermeasure: Measure the true capture-to-onset latency tail on synchronized hardware clocks; never certify real-time safety from neural network inference benchmarks alone.

Fallacy: Software step commands deliver instantaneous physical torque.

Reinforcement learning policies trained in simulation routinely issue piecewise-constant torque or position setpoints at \(50\text{ Hz}\), implicitly assuming the physical actuator behaves as an ideal, memoryless force source. In physical reality, an electric drive is an electromechanical dynamic system governed by electromagnetic and inertial limits. Developing torque requires inner Field-Oriented Control (FOC) loops whose current rise rate is strictly bounded by stator winding electrical time constants (\(\ElectricalTimeConstant = \PhaseInductance/\PhaseResistance\)) and DC bus voltage headroom (\(\frac{\DerivativeOperator\MotorTorque}{d\TimeInstant} \le \frac{\MotorTorqueConstant(\DcBusVoltage - \BackEmfVoltage)}{\PhaseInductance}\)). Mechanically, a high-ratio gearhead amplifies rotor inertia quadratically (\(\ReflectedRotorInertia = \GearReductionRatio^2 \RotorInertia\)), imposing a steep low-pass filter on achievable link acceleration.

When a discrete tensor update demands an abrupt torque step, substantial electromagnetic effort is diverted into accelerating the spinning rotor rather than moving the external link. Transmission compliance and joint elasticity convert these high-frequency torque transients into structural vibration and limit-cycle oscillations. Simultaneously, repeated high-current corrections waste energy as resistive heat dissipation (\(\WindingLossPower = \frac{\MotorTorque^2}{\MotorTorqueConstant^2} \EffectiveLossResistance\)), rapidly burning through the actuator’s thermal margin. Systems architects must filter and shape setpoints against actual plant dynamics, validating commanded trajectories against delivered physical response rather than assuming numerical smoothness in software guarantees smooth physical execution.

Architect’s countermeasure: Bound acceleration and torque slew using measured actuator response, include electrical current rise and reflected inertia in the model, and qualify the chosen transmission and controller for the intended contact task.

Pitfall: Treating isolated component datasheets as operational system guarantees.

Hardware component datasheets report performance envelopes measured under idealized bench conditions. When a motion planner adopts a motor vendor’s continuous torque rating or an IMU vendor’s nominal noise floor as operational bounds, it ignores the harsh realities of system packaging. A motor catalog rating reflects vendor test benches (section 2.5), typically an open motor bolted to an enormous aluminum heatsink in still air at \(25\text{ }^\circ\text{C}\), not a sealed robotic joint housing tucked inside an unventilated composite limb on a chassis dissipating heat from a 60 W application processor.

In physical deployment, an actuator’s continuous torque is strictly constrained by steady-state heat dissipation up to the winding’s engineering ceiling \(\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling\), so the installed rating is equation 7 evaluated at the ceiling rather than the insulation rating: \[\ContinuousTorque = \MotorTorqueConstant \sqrt{\frac{\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling - \AmbientTemperature}{\ElectricalResistance(\EngineeringTemperatureCeiling) \ThermalResistance}}\] Given a 140 °C engineering ceiling, shifting the motor from the vendor’s 25 °C bench into the 40 °C interior of an enclosed chassis strips away 6.7 percent of continuous torque. Because thermal dissipation scales inversely with the square root of thermal resistance (\(1/\sqrt{\ThermalResistance}\)), eliminating forced convection and heatsink mass degrades capacity even further: doubling \(\ThermalResistance\) inside the arm housing cuts total usable continuous torque by 34.1 percent relative to the catalog rating. If trajectory planners treat catalog continuous torque as achievable duty, persistent copper losses (\(\WindingLossPower = \frac{\MotorTorque^2}{\MotorTorqueConstant^2} \EffectiveLossResistance\)) will exceed heat dissipation rates, triggering thermal cutouts or permanently degrading winding insulation.

Sensors suffer the same systemic degradation: inertial measurement units and optical encoders calibrated on quiet granite surface plates suffer severe vibration rectification, thermal drift, and mechanical aliasing once bolted near high-frequency switching inverters and planetary gearsets. System limits must reflect empirical measurements collected on the fully assembled machine under peak operational stress rather than pristine standalone datasheets.

Architect’s countermeasure: Calibrate actuator continuous torque ceilings and sensor noise profiles on fully assembled, enclosed hardware across the entire operational temperature envelope rather than copying open-bench catalog numbers into planner configuration files.

Pitfall: Treating dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) as a transparent software optimization.

In server computing, DVFS operates as an invisible operating-system service that reduces clock frequency and core voltages to prevent silicon junction overtemperature. In an embodied system, however, DVFS directly degrades the real-time control contract. Consider a mobile manipulator whose high-current drive motors heat a shared structural chassis during intense maneuvering. As heat conducts into the compute bay, the application processor throttles its clock frequency to avoid thermal shutdown.

The system kernel treats clock throttling as an isolated power-management decision. However, a downclocked vision backbone adds observation age, and observation age at speed is unmodeled blind travel before the safety monitor can command braking. Section 2.8.2 prices that travel on the running machine. Although thermal throttling successfully shields the processor silicon, the vehicle’s dynamic stopping envelope silently expands beyond calibrated safety margins.

Architect’s countermeasure: Feed SoC thermal and clock-frequency telemetry directly into the motion safety monitor, dynamically derating maximum travel speed whenever compute throttling inflates perception age.

Pitfall: Scaling operational velocity without recalculating quadratic braking distances and thermal dissipation.

Facility operators frequently seek to increase robot throughput by raising maximum cruise velocity in software configurations. This operational change couples two punishing physical nonlinearities. First, the braking term grows with the square of speed, which is why Case 3 of figure 2 breaches the clear distance at 2 m/s.

Second, absorbing the kinetic energy of the faster machine demands large dynamic braking currents that dump heat directly into motor stator windings and drive electronics via Joule heating (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\)). An isolated emergency stop performed during commissioning may pass thermal checks when the machine starts cold. However, when an autonomous vehicle repeats that higher-speed duty cycle across an eight-hour shift, winding temperatures compound across successive maneuvers, rapidly outstripping the chassis thermal dissipation budget. System planners must treat operating velocity as a gated admission parameter: any commanded speed increase requires re-evaluating both the expanded quadratic stopping envelope and the cumulative multi-cycle thermal duty budget.

Architect’s countermeasure: Treat operating speed as an admission parameter that re-evaluates both quadratic stopping envelopes and multi-cycle thermal accumulation before granting velocity increases.

Fallacy: Compute accelerators and motor inverters operate on independent, ideal power rails.

Software architectures typically treat accelerator power consumption and motor drive power as decoupled subsystems, verifying each against isolated power supply ratings. In physical mobile machines, however, both systems draw from a shared, unregulated DC battery bus.

Consider a scenario where a heavy neural network vision burst coincides precisely with the drive motors accelerating to push the mobile base over an uneven threshold. On the shared 47.7 V electrical bus, this coincident current surge demands a combined 70 A step. Drawn through the 60 mΩ source resistance of cell, harness, and switch, this sudden draw induces a sustained 4.2 V voltage sag. That sag consumes nearly half of the 9 V voltage margin separating a depleted battery pack from the 33 V UVLO threshold of the application processor’s DC-DC converter, even before harness inductance and bulk capacitor discharge deepen the dynamic transient. A step of 150 A, or the identical current demand drawn from an aging or cold battery with sufficiently elevated internal impedance, reaches the UVLO threshold and can interrupt the primary processor’s supply.

If safety microcontrollers and brake solenoids share that unbuffered power rail, the resulting brownout crashes the safety supervisor simultaneously. The machine is plunged into the uncontrolled, unpowered fail states described in section 2.6 while carrying significant physical momentum, rather than executing an orderly, controlled deceleration. Software power budgets must acknowledge electrical coupling at the physical bus. System qualification demands testing coincident worst-case compute and motor loads at minimum battery state of charge, while ensuring safety permission logic and electro-mechanical brakes operate on an isolated, held-up power rail (section 2.8).

Architect’s countermeasure: Isolate the permission logic and brake releases, then qualify both their hold-up and the physical stopping response under coincident motor and compute load at minimum battery state of charge. A powered stop also requires the drive rail and energy-absorption path to remain usable.

Fallacy: Passing isolated single-variable bench tests guarantees physical survival under compound operational stress.

Development teams often qualify physical AI systems through single-variable stress testing: a robotic manipulator verifies maximum payload capacity inside a \(20^\circ\text{C}\) air-conditioned laboratory, then undergoes thermal validation inside a \(45^\circ\text{C}\) environmental chamber with an unloaded gripper. Neither benchmark, however, validates whether the manipulator can lift maximum payload in high ambient heat while operating from a depleted battery pack.

Physical failure modes couple nonlinearly across subsystem domains. Under simultaneous operational stress, manipulating maximum payload demands sustained high motor currents that dramatically increase resistive winding losses (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\)). An ambient temperature of \(45^\circ\text{C}\) severely compresses the thermal gradient required to reject that heat to the environment, accelerating stator insulation degradation. Concurrently, a low battery state of charge depresses DC bus voltage, stripping away the voltage headroom required to overcome motor back-EMF (\(\BackEmfVoltage\)) and severely degrading current slew rates (\(\frac{d\TorqueProducingCurrent}{d\TimeInstant}\)). An arm that moves smoothly in isolated tests may experience joint stall, severe tracking errors, or premature thermal shutdown under their compound interaction. Hardware and software qualification cannot rely on orthogonal single-variable test sweeps. Engineering teams must validate boundary envelopes on fully integrated machines subjected to simultaneous peak stresses across payload, thermal ambient, battery state of charge, and control frequency.

Architect’s countermeasure: Test boundary envelopes under worst-case simultaneous physical stress (maximum payload, elevated ambient temperature, depleted battery, and high-frequency actuation) to catch multi-budget coupling failures.

2.10 Summary

Every motion plan in physical AI must ultimately satisfy hardware invariants. Five coupled budgets translate a high-level trajectory request into concrete engineering constraints: observation age, stopping distance, delivered torque, winding temperature, and supply voltage. Because these budgets are tightly coupled, no single limit can be evaluated in isolation. Consider the warehouse robot operating on a 48 V drive bus: while the sustained electrical draw clears the converter lockout even from a depleted battery pack, the stopping budget rests on fragile assumptions. The machine’s credible deceleration margin is narrow and holds only while the drive current limit stays at the motor’s peak. Furthermore, its traction model depends on dynamic wheel loading, and its anti-tip threshold lacks empirical center-of-mass validation. Isolating and sustaining the safety permission path on a dedicated capacitor-backed supply protects digital control availability during bus transients. Certifying the physical safety envelope, however, still requires verifying the actual drive power or passive braking dynamics available during an electrical fault.

To make these boundaries enforceable, a physical limit cannot be treated as a bare scalar. Instead, systems engineers structure each boundary as a limit record that documents valid operating conditions, measurement uncertainty, safety margins, runtime detection mechanisms, and calibration provenance. On the reference mobile manipulator traveling at its 1.5 m/s drive limit, mechanical stopping dynamics consume 742.5 mm of the 1.10 m clearance distance. That figure already includes the localization bound and protective clearance, so the remaining 357.5 mm is a design budget. It covers the delay terms the body does not own (observation age, chunk lease, permission tick, and bus cycle), stopping-profile shaping, and the tracking bound that later chapters add. These are the first three rows of the stopping budget, which The warehouse mobile manipulator's stopping budget completes term by term, with the chapter that prices each one. These analytical values guide initial system architecture, while physical characterization of the fully assembled robot establishes the empirical envelopes that production controllers enforce across the governing relations of all five budgets (table 5).

Key Takeaways: The physical body and invariant budgets
  • Age begins at physical transduction: The data freshness budget spans the entire timeline from physical photon or contact arrival to mechanical force delivery. Because stage latencies vary dynamically, an end-to-end tail percentile is never the simple sum of individual stage percentiles. Robust software must preserve hardware capture timestamps, account for downstream actuation lag, and reject any action proposal whose underlying sensory evidence will be stale when the actuator responds.
  • Speed sets the stopping envelope: The clearance quadratic in speed determines an admissible velocity ceiling only while runtime state, stopping mechanics, and credible deceleration satisfy its mathematical premises. In turn, credible deceleration cannot exceed the strictest bound among tire traction, vehicle tip-over thresholds, and available motor braking torque.
  • Gearing trades torque for reflected inertia: A transmission multiplies actuator torque linearly by the gear ratio \(\GearReductionRatio\), but reflects rotor inertia back to the joint by \(\GearReductionRatio^2\). Because physical motion also depends on current rise times, inverter voltage headroom, mechanical compliance, and joint friction, software acceptance of a setpoint command never guarantees physical trajectory tracking.
  • Thermal history limits available torque: Resistive copper loss (\(\WindingLossPower = \TorqueProducingCurrent^2 \EffectiveLossResistance\)) scales quadratically with current, and winding resistance increases as internal temperatures climb. Actuator burst schedules must therefore respect transient thermal peaks rather than relying on average power limits. Furthermore, reducing holding current to alleviate thermal strain requires mechanical brakes or counterbalancing mechanisms capable of supporting static gravity loads.
  • The supply rail is shared: Motor inverters and digital compute share a common DC bus. Coincident acceleration demands induce inductive rail droop, rapidly depleting voltage headroom above converter undervoltage lockouts, while unclamped regenerative braking can elevate bus voltage and damage inverter stages. An isolated, capacitor-backed power domain preserves safety permission logic through transient brownouts. However, completing a controlled emergency stop still requires sufficient braking torque, energy absorption, and stopping clearance; otherwise, the machine must rely entirely on a qualified unpowered response.
  • A limit is a record, not a number: An engineering threshold safely governs an execution decision only when coupled with its valid operating regime, measurement uncertainty, safety margin, fault detector, and calibration provenance. Moreover, these limits lapse as hardware degrades (rising thermal resistance, increasing battery internal impedance, transmission backlash wear), so they require continuous lifecycle monitoring and recalibration.

Every budget in this chapter ends the same way, as a number the permission path can compare against a proposal before that proposal becomes current in a winding. That comparison is how the first law, irreversibility (principle 1), enters the machine as engineering rather than as a warning. Work delivered across the causal boundary cannot be recalled, so each command must remain subject to refusal before it produces physical work. Refusing it takes a limit that states its value, the conditions under which it holds, how uncertain it is, and what detects its violation. In operation, each relevant limit record must support the proposed maneuver under its actual conditions. On the running machine, the electrical budget passes only its sustained screen, and the arm’s illustrative thermal model gives a burst lasting tens of seconds. The credible deceleration rests on peak drive current, a dry floor, and an unmeasured center of gravity, and every stopping calculation inherits those conditions. When a budget looks comfortable, the first question is which condition of its record the next maneuver could violate, and the second is whether a detector would notice before the stop is needed.

2.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The freshness budget rests on foundations established well before the advent of digital control. Nyquist (1928) quantified the transmission capacity of telegraph channels relative to available bandwidth. Shannon (1949) formalized the sampling theorem in the form used throughout this chapter: a continuous signal band-limited below half the sampling frequency is completely determined by discrete samples, whereas unattenuated higher-frequency dynamics fold back into the passband as aliasing distortion. Clock synchronization subsequently migrated from software operating systems directly into physical networking silicon. The Precision Time Protocol (IEEE 2020a) and its profile for time-sensitive Ethernet (IEEE 2020b) capture packet timestamps at the physical layer interface. They allow an embodied system to preserve each sensor measurement’s capture timestamp under bounded capture and clock-synchronization uncertainty as it travels through the compute fabric.

Nyquist, Harry. 1928. “Certain Topics in Telegraph Transmission Theory.” Transactions of the American Institute of Electrical Engineers 47 (2): 617–44. https://doi.org/10.1109/T-AIEE.1928.5055024.
Shannon, Claude E. 1949. “Communication in the Presence of Noise.” Proceedings of the IRE 37 (1): 10–21. https://doi.org/10.1109/JRPROC.1949.232969.
Asada, Haruhiko, and Takeo Kanade. 1983. “Design of Direct-Drive Mechanical Arms.” ASME Journal of Vibration, Acoustics, Stress, and Reliability in Design 105 (3): 312–16. https://doi.org/10.1115/1.3269094.
Seok, Sangok, Albert Wang, Meng Yee Chuah, Dong Jin Hyun, JaeJun Lee, David M. Otten, Jeffrey H. Lang, and Sangbae Kim. 2015. “Design Principles for Energy-Efficient Legged Locomotion and Implementation on the MIT Cheetah Robot.” IEEE/ASME Transactions on Mechatronics 20 (3): 1117–29.

Modern actuator architectures trace back to two distinct engineering lineages. The first lineage eliminated mechanical transmissions entirely to maximize backdrivability. Asada and Kanade (1983) developed early direct-drive robot arms to bypass the nonlinear friction, backlash, and torque ripple inherent in high-ratio gearboxes. Three decades later, the MIT Cheetah quadruped revisited this architecture with the quasi-direct drive of section 2.4.5, coupling high-torque-density motors with low-ratio single-stage planetary gearsets to keep most of that transparency while adding impact resilience (Seok et al. 2015; Wensing et al. 2017). The second lineage treated transmission compliance not as an unwanted defect, but as an explicit design variable. Musser (1959) patented the strain wave gear (harmonic drive), whose compact form factor and high reduction ratio trade off torsional stiffness for torque density. To analyze these mechanics, Spong (1987) introduced the flexible-joint model, demonstrating how elastic coupling dynamically decouples motor inertia from the load link. Moving beyond rigid position tracking, Hogan (1985) reframed physical contact as an exchange of energy governed by a programmed mechanical impedance. Building directly on this concept, Pratt and Williamson (1995) introduced series elastic actuators, deliberately placing an elastic element between the gearbox and the output link so that spring deflection provides reliable force sensing while shielding gear teeth from shock impacts. Learned policies successfully mastered these compliant systems once Hwangbo et al. (2019) replaced idealized torque sources in simulation with compact neural networks identified directly from physical hardware.

Thermal and electrical budgets descend from classical electrical machine design and power systems engineering. Montsinger (1930) demonstrated that operational limits for electrical machines should be governed by winding insulation temperature rather than instantaneous current thresholds alone. Dakin (1948) modeled this insulation degradation as a chemical reaction obeying Arrhenius kinetics, proving that thermal aging accelerates exponentially with operating temperature. Modern international standards such as IEC 60085 (International Electrotechnical Commission 2007) still formalize this trade-off between operating temperature and motor operational lifespan. Similarly, standardized stop categories and drive safety functions (International Electrotechnical Commission 2016a, 2016b) codify decades of industrial safety practice for removing power from moving physical mass under fault conditions. Physical AI introduces a new challenge: learned policy proposers operate without built-in guarantees regarding these physical budgets. Deterministic safety monitors must therefore enforce these boundaries directly, translating thermal, electrical, and kinematic constraints into explicit limit records checked along the permission path.

International Electrotechnical Commission. 2007. IEC 60085:2007: Electrical Insulation, Thermal Evaluation and Designation. International Electrotechnical Commission.
International Electrotechnical Commission. 2016a. IEC 60204-1:2016: Safety of Machinery, Electrical Equipment of Machines, Part 1: General Requirements. International Electrotechnical Commission.
International Electrotechnical Commission. 2016b. IEC 61800-5-2:2016: Adjustable Speed Electrical Power Drive Systems, Part 5-2: Safety Requirements, Functional. International Electrotechnical Commission.
  • Mechanics and actuation from first principles (Lynch and Park 2017). A comprehensive derivation of rigid-body dynamics, kinematic chains, gearing, and contact mechanics.
  • Impedance control (Hogan 1985). The seminal paper establishing contact manipulation as dynamic interaction rather than pure position or force control, introducing the virtual spring-damper formulations used to regulate interaction forces.
  • Flexible-joint dynamics (Spong 1987). The analytical foundation modeling elastic transmission compliance as a torsional spring, demonstrating why motor-side encoders cannot certify link motion or guarantee end-effector trajectory tracking.
  • Series elastic actuators (Pratt and Williamson 1995). The deliberate introduction of mechanical compliance to trade control bandwidth for impact tolerance, energy storage, and accurate force sensing.
  • Proprioceptive actuator design (Wensing et al. 2017). Details how low-ratio transmissions paired with high-torque electric motors allow motor current to serve as a reliable contact-force signal, highlighting the associated thermal limits and torque-density trade-offs.
  • Learned actuator models (Hwangbo et al. 2019). Demonstrates that compact neural networks trained on minutes of empirical hardware data close the reality gap between idealized simulator torques and nonlinear physical actuators.
Lynch, Kevin M, and Frank C Park. 2017. Modern Robotics: Mechanics, Planning, and Control. Cambridge University Press.
Hogan, Neville. 1985. “Impedance Control: An Approach to Manipulation: Part i–Theory, Part II–Implementation, Part III–Applications.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 107 (1): 1–24.
Spong, Mark W. 1987. “Modeling and Control of Elastic Joint Robots.” Journal of Dynamic Systems, Measurement, and Control 109 (4): 310–19.
Pratt, Gill A., and Matthew M. Williamson. 1995. “Series Elastic Actuators.” Proceedings of the 1995 IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS) 1: 399–406. https://doi.org/10.1109/IROS.1995.525827.
Wensing, Patrick M., Albert Wang, Sangok Seok, David Otten, Jeffrey H. Lang, and Sangbae Kim. 2017. “Proprioceptive Actuator Design in the MIT Cheetah: Impact Mitigation and High-Bandwidth Physical Interaction for Dynamic Legged Robots.” IEEE Transactions on Robotics 33 (3): 509–22.
Hwangbo, Jemin, Joonho Lee, Alexey Dosovitskiy, Dario Bellicoso, Vassilios Tsounis, Vladlen Koltun, and Marco Hutter. 2019. “Learning Agile and Dynamic Motor Skills for Legged Robots.” Science Robotics 4 (26): eaau5872. https://doi.org/10.1126/scirobotics.aau5872.

What’s Next: From body limits to brain proposals
Every budget in this chapter now ends as a limit record, and all five can hold while the machine still does nothing useful. At the pick station the observation can be fresh, the stop inside its clearance, the windings cool, and the bus above lockout, yet nothing in the body says which object on the conveyor is the mug or where its handle is. That judgment comes from a learned proposer, and The Cognitive Brain opens with the cost of supplying it in time. A model large enough to recognize unfamiliar objects streams its weights through the same onboard processor that sensing and control depend on, so it renews its proposal far more slowly than the servo loops run. The chapter derives the two-speed brain that the machine card commits to, a slow intent model above a fast chunk policy, and shows why an action chunk keeps the arm supplied without keeping its evidence fresh. The freshness deadline and stopping budget derived here are the windows those proposals must arrive within, and The Nervous System joins the two, admitting each chunk only under a bounded lease that the body’s limit records define.
